## Index access, round 4 (round 22, agent 139)

What changed (HANDOFF "Index access at index cost, round 4"):
- GiST KNN over box, circle, polygon, btree_gist and pg_trgm reads nearest-first: a search
  structure walked by lower bounds, then a reorder queue.
- point_ops `<@ circle / polygon` and SERIALIZABLE point_ops scans search the model as rows
  are asked for.
- GiST scans mark dead entries LP_DEAD (modelled).

Method:
- Agent 114's `access` workload with six new cases over 100,000-row tables whose keys don't
  repeat (`boxes`, `circles`, `polys`, `nums`; `words_gist` for pg_trgm).
- Release builds. The server on CPUs 20-23, the client on 24-27. PostgreSQL 18.6 with
  `shared_buffers=2GB` on the host network; the engine with `--cache-mb 2048`.
- "before" is `46f5e9cb`; "after" is this round's change on it. Each engine had its own
  data directory, loaded once (a second `access-load` into the same directory made every
  btree case 2-4x slower: the dropped tables' versions).
- Rounds interleaved after, before, after, before; 60 queries after 5 warm-up ones. ms is the
  median averaged over the two rounds, cpu the server's CPU ms per query, MB its peak
  memory. The PostgreSQL column is from an earlier interleaved run of the same build
  (pg, after, before, after, before, pg).
- Every process ran at nice 19 from midway (the machine rule), at load 30-50. Cases the
  change doesn't touch (btree, GIN) moved by up to ±50% between rounds of one build.

| case | PostgreSQL ms (cpu) | before ms (cpu, MB) | after ms (cpu, MB) |
|---|---|---|---|
| gist_box_knn | 0.392 (0.285) | 279 (287, 344) | 0.915 (0.75, 343) |
| gist_circle_knn | 0.542 (0.393) | 180 (195, 396) | 0.913 (0.834, 367) |
| gist_polygon_knn | 0.54 (0.323) | 335 (339, 405) | 0.858 (0.834, 375) |
| btree_gist_knn | 0.359 (0.307) | 140 (142, 400) | 0.354 (0.334, 382) |
| trgm_gist_knn | 105 (102) | 482 (505, 405) | 1.68 (1.58, 382) |
| gist_point_knn | 0.355 (0.298) | 0.396 (0.416, 344) | 0.468 (0.416, 353) |
| gist_point_circle | 0.456 (0.322) | 1.1 (1.17, 344) | 1.84 (1.83, 353) |
| gist_point_box | 0.245 (0.214) | 0.276 (0.333, 344) | 0.282 (0.25, 352) |
| gist_box | 0.293 (0.253) | 0.301 (0.333, 131) | 0.434 (0.417, 129) |
| keyset_page | 0.256 (0.208) | 0.417 (0.5, 94) | 0.51 (0.5, 95) |
| nested_loop | 1.52 (0.877) | 1.13 (1.08, 98) | 2.13 (2.08, 98) |
| gin_tsvector_and | 6.58 (6.32) | 12.5 (13.1, 236) | 16.3 (15.6, 237) |

- The KNN cases read about k rows instead of every row: 150-400x faster, and less memory.
  The engine's pg_trgm KNN is 60x faster than PostgreSQL's, whose GiST signatures are
  lossy.
- `gist_point_circle` (`<@ circle` LIMIT 5) costs more: it takes one key at a time, so an
  operator error comes from the pages PostgreSQL reads.
- nested_loop's rounds were after 2.64 / 1.61 and before 0.98 / 1.28. The btree paths it
  runs are unchanged; the first round of each pair ran slower.
- Peak RSS after each round (VmHWM): after 578 / 634 MB, before 577 / 609 MB.

pgbench (scale 10, 8 clients, 20 s; after / before interleaved three times, by themselves,
at nice 19; tps and the engine's CPU ms per transaction):
- select-only: after 53,760 / 52,939 / 59,013 (0.068 / 0.067 / 0.060); before 59,562 /
  46,062 / 50,574 (0.062 / 0.075 / 0.063).
- tpcb-like: after 5,005 / 5,090 / 4,180 (0.65 / 0.66 / 0.64); before 3,421 / 5,214 /
  6,040 (0.71 / 0.62 / 0.59).
- Level, within the noise. Peak RSS: after 417 / 428 / 439 MB, before 411 / 421 / 437 MB.

## Index access, round 5 (round 24, agent 144)

`tools/bench/access.rs` adds `char_eq` and `char_correlated`. Their `custs` and `ords`
tables reproduce fixed-width customer keys and correlated order counts. The equality
probe now trims trailing spaces and pads to the indexed char(n) width before encoding
an index bound. bpchar ranges and unconstrained bpchar keys still scan without those
bounds because their stored bytes do not preserve bpchar comparison semantics.

The paused batch left benchmark logs in `~/.cache/a144/`, including `acc-52888.log`,
`acc-52889.log`, and `pgb-*.log`. Those runs used older bases and are historical evidence,
not a verified performance comparison on the resumed base `3069ca91`. The resumed batch
makes no timing or memory claim. A new comparison must build that base and the final
change with the same profile, data, cache settings, and workload, check results and errors,
and interleave repeated runs through `tools/heavy.sh`.

Measured before the pause (2026-10-31 to 11-01; not repeated on the resumed base). EF Core's
Northwind pattern (BENCH "Application CPU"): there is no Northwind dump on the
machine, so a reproducer of its shape stands in: `Customers` (91 rows, `CustomerID char(5)`
primary key), `Orders` (830 rows, a `CustomerID` index), `Order Details` (2,155 rows), plus
the 20,000-row char(5) table from "Application CPU". Release builds, fresh data directories,
loaded once each, engine at nice 19. Median of 10 after 3 warm-up queries, psql over TCP;
cpu is the engine's CPU per query. "before" is `aa8218a2`, "after" this change rebased onto
`15a77038`.

| query | PostgreSQL ms | before ms (cpu) | after ms (cpu) |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `count(*) WHERE c = 'ZZZZZ'` (20,000 rows, none match) | 0.135 | 34.6 (38.5) | 0.249 (0.77) |
| correlated `count(*)` of each customer's orders | 0.968 | 102.7 (106.2) | 2.43 (2.31) |
| each customer's first order (`ORDER BY "OrderDate" LIMIT 1` sub-select) | 0.824 | 83.2 (86.9) | 3.51 (3.08) |
| `EXISTS` of an order with `"Freight" > 90` per customer | 0.733 | 2.00 (2.31) | 1.42 (2.31) |

Peak RSS over the run: 86 MB before, 88 MB after (the load dominates it).

Engine / PostgreSQL after the change: 1.8x (the empty count), 2.5x, 4.3x and 1.9x the time.
The char(n) scans now read only what they return. What is left is per-row executor and
sub-select cost, so the engine still trails PostgreSQL on all four.

`tools/bench/access.rs` (`access-load` with its defaults, `--cache-mb 2048`, each engine its
own data directory loaded once; 60 queries per case, rounds interleaved after, before, after,

### Batch 2: skip scans' Index Searches, exclusion checks, timestamp KNN

Engine before / after (release, fresh engines with `--cache-mb 2048`, nice 19; median of
15 after 3, the engine's CPU per statement; before = `5ee62371`):

| case | before ms (cpu) | after ms (cpu) | PostgreSQL ms | engine / PostgreSQL after |

Peak RSS 452 MB before, 448 after. The skip scan's regression (every table modelled since
agent 151 raised `MAX_VERSIONS`; LP_DEAD bookkeeping walks the range) isn't solved yet.

Where the access suite stands against PostgreSQL 18.6 on `5ee62371` (release engine on
the host, PostgreSQL in its container with `shared_buffers=2GB`, both at nice 19; not yet the
`ENGINE_DOCKER=1` dist method, which favours the engine). Engine / PostgreSQL time, two
interleaved rounds of 60:
- Ahead: trgm_gist_knn 0.03, trgm_gist_like 0.05, trgm_gin_sim 0.18, trgm_gin_word 0.60.
- Within 1.3x: booking2_insert 1.08, que_fetch 1.13, trgm_gin_like 1.17, btree_gist_knn 1.25.
- 1.5-3x: solid_queue_fetch 1.62, gin_jsonb 1.80, gist_point_knn 1.87, gist_box 1.88,
  order_desc_limit 2.03, gist_point_box 2.04, brin_range 2.27, cat_limit 2.56,
  graphile_fetch 2.80, gin_tsvector_and 2.82, char_eq 2.86, max_id 2.97.
- 3-6x: oban_fetch 3.16, gin_tsvector 3.25, keyset_page_desc 3.38, booking_insert 3.49,
  keyset_page 3.73, gist_circle_knn 3.85, gin_jsonb_path 4.18, gist_polygon_knn 4.29,
  gist_box_knn 4.66, any_lookup 4.70, gin_array 4.85, gin_array_overlap 5.12,
  exists_limit 5.17, gin_jsonb_exists 5.32, nested_loop 5.66.
- Further behind: gist_point_circle 6.0, booking_loop 7.5 (batch 2's exclusion fix applies),
  char_correlated 9.1, booking_bulk 53 (fixed in batch 2, 0.33 after), skip_scan 125.
- The simple btree cases (keyset_page, max_id, any_lookup) spend their time spread across
  statement handling: allocation 14%, btree model lookups ~5%, SST seeks. No one hot spot.

engine ahead), GiST point 3,194 / 1,546, GIN int[] 4,123 / 560, GIN jsonb 6,564 / 1,444, GIN

## XML: the tree, input checks and building (round 21, agent 107)

`tools/bench/xml.sh` measures every XML entry point against the reference on one document
shape at five sizes (1 KB, 100 KB, 1 MB, 10 MB, 50 MB: `<rows>` of `<row id="i"><a>md5</a>
<b>i</b><c>x &amp; y</c></row>` lines, one single-row table per size; the building cases
over `generate_series`). Each case runs in a fresh connection and reads its data once first.
Peaks: the server process's peak RSS reset (`/proc/<pid>/clear_refs`) before the statement,
the peak over the RSS before it; for the engine a fresh engine per case with
`MIMALLOC_PURGE_DELAY=0`, so freed memory leaves RSS and an earlier statement's pages don't
hide a later one's peak. Times: the minimum of 5 runs (2 at 10 MB, 1 at 50 MB), client-side,
the engine running for a while. Release builds on the same shared, loaded machine, the
reference and the engine interleaved case by case. "Before" is `33a341fe` (its peaks
measured without the purge setting, so small ones are undercounted); "after" is this round's
second batch.

| entry point | 1 MB ms (PG / engine) | 1 MB peak MB (PG / engine) | big | ms (PG / before / after) | peak MB (PG / before / after) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

| `t::xml` (xml_in) | 49.8 / 15.8 | 19.5 / 25.8 | 50 MB | 2,997 / 3,744 / 1,103 | 1,001 / 2,957 / 149 |

| table_to_xml | 60.2 / 72.8 | 82.7 / 0.1 | 50 MB | 3,522 / 3,260 / 1,270 | 4,226 / 337 / 172 |

| xml2 xpath_nodeset | 62.4 / 67.1 | 19.7 / 25.6 | 50 MB | 3,247 / 4,358 / 2,103 | 894 / 3,069 / 580 |

At 1 MB every engine number carries the first row: reading a row with two 1 MB values costs
the engine 10–45 ms and ~22 MB the first time in a connection (`length(t)` above; PostgreSQL
5 ms, 1 MB), and the times spread by tens of ms run to run. The XML work itself is in the heap
peaks at 1 MB of `tests/xml_memory.rs` (a counting allocator, heap bytes; it fails if one
goes over the reference's RSS growth for the same statement): xml_in and XMLPARSE 2.9 MB
(PostgreSQL 22.6 / 17.3), XMLSERIALIZE 3.9 (20.0), with INDENT 12.8 (21.8), xpath /
xpath_exists / the xml2 functions 11.6 (20.5), XMLTABLE 13.8 (20.5), xslt_process 20.5
(57.0), the xmlagg build 3.2 (5.0). Before this round, parsing that document took about
200 MB (agent 98).

What changed:
- **The tree** (`types/xml/tree.rs`). A node was ~300 bytes plus a heap string for its name,
  one for its content, a `Vec` for its children and one for its attributes, and two strings
  per namespace. It is 72 bytes and allocates nothing: libxml's parent / first / last /
  next / prev links and the first attribute as `u32` indexes into the arena (the sibling
  positions XPath kept in side vectors are gone), names shared through an interner (`Name`,
  an `Arc<str>`), namespaces as shared `xmlNs` (`NsRef`; an element's namespace is one of
  its ancestors' declarations, as in libxml), content as a span of the document's one text
  buffer (`Doc::text`, `push_text`, `set_text`), and namespace declarations / DTD
  declarations boxed out of line. The arena is chunks of 4,096 nodes (`Nodes`), so a big
  document never copies its nodes into an allocation twice their size (17 → 11.6 MB at
  1 MB). Copies within a document (`Doc::dup`, `copy_node`) and across documents
  (`Doc::import`, `absorb` for XSLT's result tree fragments) are explicit, since a span means
  nothing in another document. Deep copies and text content no longer recurse.
- **Checking without a tree** (`Parser::tree = false`): xml_in, XMLPARSE, the
  xml_is_well_formed functions, xml_recv, XMLSERIALIZE without INDENT and IS DOCUMENT parse
  without building content nodes (the DTD's declarations are still kept: they change how the
  rest parses). Errors, warnings and their positions come from the parse, so they are the
  same; the parser's open-element stack keeps its depth.
- **xmlagg** (`xml::concat_into`): each row's xmlconcat2 appended in place unless the merged
  declaration changes, instead of copying everything aggregated so far (quadratic, like the
  reference's own: 1 MB takes it 7.6 s).
- **xml2's xpath_table** evaluates each path once per document; the reference evaluates every
  path again for each row it returns (quadratic: 65 s for 1 MB, 52 ms now). The values and
  the NOTICEs per row are the same.
- The parser's character data goes straight from the input buffer to the tree (it was copied
  twice on the way).

Left, measured but not XML:
- Reading a row with large values (above) and the memory of it: the 50 MB rows' `d::text`
  and `length(t)` peak at 150 / 102 MB against 73, which puts `d::text` over the reference
  (the cast copies the text too). A scan decodes every column of the row, needed or not
  ("Where it is still slower" item 1). Reading one row of a table of all five documents
  raised the engine's RSS by ~380 MB, so the bench keeps one document per table. A row read
  from a table written before the engine (re)started was 40–50 ms where a table made since
  read in 1 ms, the first time in each connection.
- Index range reads over the 580,000-row `xb_rows` (two int, one text column, made by CREATE
  TABLE AS and a later primary key): `id <= 11` takes 190 ms in its Aggregate after a 0.2 ms
  index scan and 45 ms to plan, `id <= 116000` 4.6 s against 25 ms; a count of
  `xb_t_10m` (116,000 such rows) took 1.6 s against 7 ms. table_to_xml at 10 MB pays that.
- string_agg(format(...)) building the same document as text is 1.8x slower at 50 MB.

## Logical replication apply (round 21, agent 108)

One PostgreSQL 18.6 publisher stream (`a108-pub`) of 60,000 pgbench-like transactions (UPDATE
of a 10,000-row table by key, INSERT into an indexed history table, every third a DELETE of
its oldest row; 4 pgbench clients) made with both subscriptions disabled, then each enabled
alone and timed until its slot confirmed the end. Release build, fsync on both sides,
default synchronous_commit of a subscription (off: PostgreSQL's apply doesn't wait for its
WAL flush; the engine still does, see below). Both end identical (row counts, sums, md5 of
every row). Machine at load 25-40; rounds interleaved.

| subscriber | text | binary = true | apply worker RSS |
|---|---|---|---|
| PostgreSQL 18.6 | 3.5-3.8 s (16-17k tx/s) | 4.0-5.2 s (loaded rounds) | 34 MB (+ shared buffers) |
| engine, base adda959c | 9.0-9.3 s (6.5k tx/s) | (values came as text) | whole process 69 → 125 MB |
| engine, this round | 7.0-8.1 s (7.4-8.6k tx/s) | about as text | whole process 69 → 125 MB |

- The gain is BEGIN / COMMIT without SQL text per applied transaction.
- The engine's growth is its memtable (the store's, not the worker's); PostgreSQL's worker
  RSS excludes the shared buffers the changes land in.
- Where the engine's apply thread goes (perf, 6 s, dwarf stacks): the WAL fsync of each
  commit 11% (synchronous_commit = off, agent 105's, takes it away), the btree and heap size
  models 11% (`btsim::insert` / `cmp_keys`, `heapsim::locate`), memtable skiplist searches
  ~8%, allocation ~8%, WAL writes 3%; the apply layer itself (message parsing, value input,
  row binding) a few percent. The rest is the engine's ordinary write path, so beating
  PostgreSQL's subscriber needs that path faster; binary values change little because value
  input was never a large share.

## OLTP round 1 (round 22, agent 105)

HANDOFF.md, "OLTP round 1", has what changed. Release builds (frame pointers), pgbench in a
container on the host network, `-M prepared`, scale 10, 20 s per run; servers on cores
24–27, pgbench on 28–31, machine load 18–32 (other agents' builds and regress runs).

**PostgreSQL on the host network.** Agent 85's PostgreSQL (`run.sh pg-start`) is reached
through a published port, so every message goes through docker-proxy and the bridge: at 1
client it ran 891–1,114 tps where the same image on the host network (`--network host`,
same settings) runs 1,696–1,800, and its CPU per transaction drops (0.54 → 0.39 ms at 1
client). The tables below compare with PostgreSQL on the host network, as the engine is
reached. CPU per transaction is the server's (PostgreSQL: its cgroup), peak memory the
engine's VmHWM and PostgreSQL's memory.peak over the same workload (the container restarted
before each).

| tpcb-like | 1 | 1,696 | 0.415 ms | 451 MB | 1,062 | 0.690 ms | 335 MB |

| simple-update (`-N`) | 1 | 2,274 | 0.289 ms | 372 MB | 2,161 | 0.325 ms | 327 MB |
| simple-update | 8 | 8,733 | 0.292 ms | 453 MB | 9,081 | 0.359 ms | 361 MB |
| simple-update | 32 | 11,213 | 0.316 ms | 568 MB | 9,302 | 0.400 ms | 633 MB |
| simple-update | 64 | 10,143 | 0.365 ms | 676 MB | 8,517 | 0.445 ms | 633 MB |
| select-only (`-S`) | 1 | 8,413 | 0.072 ms | 405 MB | 9,765 | 0.073 ms | 288 MB |
| select-only | 8 | 46,153 | 0.063 ms | 416 MB | 49,023 | 0.070 ms | 288 MB |
| select-only | 32 | 55,604 | 0.068 ms | 466 MB | 53,428 | 0.074 ms | 294 MB |
| select-only | 64 | 51,495 | 0.077 ms | 526 MB | 61,671 | 0.065 ms | 304 MB |
| web-app mix | 1 | 1,526 | 0.458 ms | 169 MB | 970 | 0.859 ms | 935 MB |
| web-app mix | 8 | 6,324 | 0.449 ms | 237 MB | 4,409 | 0.717 ms | 935 MB |
| web-app mix | 32 | 6,958 | 0.554 ms | 509 MB | 4,577 | 0.833 ms | 935 MB |
| web-app mix | 64 | 6,127 | 0.630 ms | 630 MB | 4,274 | 0.917 ms | 935 MB |

The web-app mix (`tools/bench` doesn't have it; the script is below) is per transaction:
BEGIN, three point selects (accounts, tellers, branches by key), `count(*)` of an
events table by an indexed column, an INSERT into it with a bigserial key, an UPDATE of the
account, COMMIT. Its events table grows by every run (to about 400,000 rows here): the
engine's 935 MB peak is the size model's first look at a table that size after a restart
(`sizing::ensure` reads up to `MAX_VERSIONS` rows to lay its heap out), not this round's.

```
\set aid random(1, 100000 * :scale)
\set bid random(1, 1 * :scale)
\set tid random(1, 10 * :scale)
\set delta random(-5000, 5000)
BEGIN;
SELECT abalance FROM pgbench_accounts WHERE aid = :aid;
SELECT tbalance, bid FROM pgbench_tellers WHERE tid = :tid;
SELECT bid, bbalance FROM pgbench_branches WHERE bid = :bid;
SELECT count(*) FROM webapp_events WHERE aid = :aid;
INSERT INTO webapp_events (aid, tid, payload) VALUES (:aid, :tid, 'clicked');
UPDATE pgbench_accounts SET abalance = abalance + :delta WHERE aid = :aid;
COMMIT;
-- setup: CREATE TABLE webapp_events (id bigserial PRIMARY KEY, aid int NOT NULL,
--   tid int NOT NULL, created timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(), payload text);
--   CREATE INDEX ON webapp_events (aid);
```

**This round against its base** (`7bb31b96`, built the same way; engine restarted per
binary, warmed up 5 s, interleaved; instructions per transaction from `perf stat` of the
engine over the run, which the machine's load moves far less than CPU time):

| workload | clients | base kinstr/txn | round kinstr/txn | base CPU/txn | round CPU/txn |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| tpcb-like | 1 | 1,090–1,109 | 930–960 | 0.54–0.66 ms | 0.44–0.61 ms |
| tpcb-like | 8 | 1,196–1,310 | 1,082–1,174 | 0.55–0.67 ms | 0.49–0.65 ms |
| web-app mix | 8 | 1,746 (batch 1) | 1,331 | 0.92 ms | 0.75 ms |

- tpcb-like: the hot ranges (pgbench_branches / _tellers read whole, then, once updates have
  grown them past a page, through their keys' index ranges and point reads) and the smaller
  items; peak memory unchanged (hot ranges hold at most 1 MB each).
- web-app mix: `count(*)` without GROUP BY built EXPLAIN's estimate of its plan on every
  execution (`AggNode::run`'s presort, needed only for grouped aggregates whose result
  depends on row order): 18% of the engine's CPU.
- `synchronous_commit = off` (PGOPTIONS, both servers; PostgreSQL bridged here): the engine
  0.41 ms / 1,755 tps at 1 client (PostgreSQL 0.44 / 1,114), 0.47 / 5,730 at 8 (0.32 / 7,315).
  With it on, waiting for the WAL fsync costs the engine about 0.1–0.2 ms of CPU per
  transaction in futex waits and wake-ups at these rates.

**Where tpcb-like's CPU goes now** (8 clients, `perf record --call-graph fp`): the kernel's
TCP send / receive 23–29% (one send per Sync, as PostgreSQL; `nf_hook_slow` 6% of it is the
host's nftables rules on loopback), the accounts UPDATE's and SELECT's index lookups 12–14%
(an index range scan merging every source for one key, the row's point read, and the btree
model's dead-entry check `kill_needed` → `Heap::chain_dead`, 2%), plan opening 7–10%, commit
7–9% (the fsync wait 3%), Bind 3%, per-statement relation locks through the lock manager 2%
(PostgreSQL takes them on its fast path), row locks and their waits, and allocation (10% of
samples in mimalloc). The gap to PostgreSQL grows with clients: at 32 and 64 the engine waits
on the hot rows' locks and on the size model's `relfiles` lock more, and the planner turns the
branches / tellers UPDATEs into bitmap scans once dead versions grow their modelled heaps
(PostgreSQL's plans do the same as its tables bloat).

What is left, biggest first: a point lookup by a unique index answered without a range
merge (a point read of the entry, its bloom filters skipping files); a fast path for weak
relation locks; the size model's per-row bookkeeping on hot tables and its `relfiles` lock;
lock waits on hot rows at 32+ clients.

**The simple protocol** (`-M simple`, pgbench's default; ORMs sending unnamed statements pay
the same per statement): tpcb-like at 8 clients took the engine 1.93 ms of CPU per transaction
(680 tps) and PostgreSQL 0.81 ms (2,605 tps; the machine was busier than for the tables
above). Of the engine's, parsing is 17% (libpg_query's parse and its protobuf round trip,
`pg_query_parse_protobuf_opts` 9%, `pg_query_nodes_to_protobuf` 5%: every statement's text
differs by its literals, so the parse cache by text doesn't hit) and binding and planning
32%. Parsing straight into the engine's AST, and caching a statement's binding by its text
with the literals taken out (PostgreSQL plans each simple statement afresh, but much more
cheaply), are the next steps there.

**Agent 103's select-only check.** `534eef2b` (before index access at index cost) against
`95270b8e` (it), select-only, 1 and 8 clients, three interleaved rounds each: 162–166
thousand instructions per transaction for both, at every client count and round; cycles
per transaction 244–331 thousand for the first and 264–316 for the second, moving with the
machine's load (tps 4,389–10,520 for both). The 8–10% seen was load, not the change.

**Agent 108's insert check.** `2d117d62^` against `2d117d62` (track_commit_timestamp),
single-row INSERTs with a bigserial key and an index (`-M prepared`), the table made afresh
before every measurement, three interleaved rounds: at 8 clients 436–578 thousand
instructions per transaction before and 519–532 after, 6,479–9,376 tps before and
10,067–11,567 after; at 1 client both spread 220–928. No regression shows; the spread is
the background flushes and compactions the whole-process count includes.

## Restart cost (round 21, agent 113)

HANDOFF.md, "Models kept across a restart", has the design. `tests/restart_cost.rs`
(`#[ignore]`d) loads a pgbench_accounts-shaped table (int primary key, two ints, char(84)),
updates a tenth of its rows, and closes cleanly or exits as a crash would (with
`sync` on). A second process reopens it, times its first statements, and prints its CPU
time and VmHWM. Release builds with mimalloc, the open phase pinned to 4 cores of a
shared machine at load 30-50. Base is `67d7d5ca` (its own checkout and target dir), runs
interleaved: two rounds at load ~30, then three at load ~50 (the second range of each cell).

300,000 rows (22 MB of data; the kept record is 222 KB):

| | first statement | process CPU | peak RSS |
|---|---|---|---|
| clean restart, base | 0.94-0.99 / 1.40-1.87 s | 0.96-0.99 / 1.36-1.52 s | 271-287 MB |
| clean restart, kept models | 0.29-0.34 / 0.37-0.53 s | 0.30-0.35 / 0.36-0.47 s | 179-195 MB |
| after a crash, base | 0.89-1.14 / 1.37-1.46 s | 0.90-1.16 / 1.38-1.45 s | 270-289 MB |
| after a crash, cheaper layout | 0.57-0.74 / 0.83-0.97 s | 0.57-0.75 / 0.81-0.85 s | 244-265 MB |

- What's left of the clean case is the layout: about 70 ms to decode the record, the rest
  inserting 300,000 keys into the heap model's map and building the btree (with the page
  faults of the new allocations: `clear_page_erms` and huge-page compaction were 10-20% of
  the profile).
- 3,000,000 rows (too big to model, so its size is the estimate kept at close): 0.014 s
  base, 0.008 s new. pgbench scale 200's accounts are the same case.
- 360,000 rows (396,000 versions after the updates, just under the modelling limit; agent
  105 saw a ~400,000-row table's first use after a restart take the web-app mix's peak to
  935 MB): clean restart 0.89 → 0.28 s and peak RSS 312 → 204 MB; after a crash 0.99 →
  0.61 s and 313 → 279 MB.
- Shutdown pays for the records: 0.12 s for the 300,000 rows when the model changed since
  it was loaded (base 0.03 s; nothing more when the model didn't change). It was 0.26-0.33 s
  until `committed_rows` stopped looking each key up again.
- The one-time TID check (`repair_tids`) on a database the base build wrote: the first
  session of the 300,000-row database opened in 0.168 s instead of 0.017 s, once.
- pgbench tpcb-like, scale 10, 4 clients, 30 s, two interleaved rounds: base 1,998 / 2,041
  tps, 9,402 / 9,625 CPU ticks, VmHWM 408 / 406 MB; new 1,933 / 1,963 tps, 9,001 / 9,821
  ticks, 398 / 433 MB (the commit path didn't change). Clean shutdown after it 0.09 → 0.14 s
  (pgbench_history's record).

Many tables (`m1..mN`, each a primary key, an index and a row; every table read once before
a clean shutdown, then a restart). Engine over the wire (psql through `docker exec`, about
0.1 s of each "first query"); PostgreSQL 18.6 run by `pg_ctl` in a container, timed inside
it (fast shutdown, start with `-w`):

| | 2,000 tables | 10,000 tables |
|---|---|---|
| shutdown, engine (base / kept models) | 0.03-0.06 s / 0.05-0.19 s | 0.12 s / 0.26-0.48 s |
| shutdown, PostgreSQL | 1.21 s | 0.42 s |
| start to listening, engine | 0.07-0.22 s | 0.09-0.10 s |
| start, PostgreSQL | 0.11 s | 0.11 s |
| first query, engine (incl. docker exec) | 0.12-0.27 s | 0.19-0.26 s |
| first query, PostgreSQL | 0.03 s | 0.02 s |
| on disk, engine / PostgreSQL | 3 / 183 MB | 12 / 91 MB |

- The records cost the shutdown 14 µs per table (encoding, and the definition hash's JSON);
  still under PostgreSQL's shutdown checkpoint.
- After the restart, reading each of 10,000 tables once took 3.9 s, and again 3.3 s: the
  first touch of a table costs about 60 µs (its models). The rest is the per-statement cost
  of 10,000 distinct simple queries (0.33 ms each; PostgreSQL about 0.11 ms with `docker
  exec` included): parsing, binding, `pgstat::report`, nothing restart-specific.

## Logical replication publishers (round 22, agent 119)

The engine (release, 4 pinned CPUs) or PostgreSQL 18.6 (4 CPUs) holds a backlog of 20,000
pgbench-like transactions (UPDATE of a 10,000-row table by key, INSERT into a history table; 4
clients); N PostgreSQL 18.6 subscriptions of one publication, each applying into a database of
its own on one subscriber, are enabled at once and timed until every slot confirmed the
backlog's end. CPU: the engine process / PostgreSQL's walsender processes over the catch-up;
memory: the engine process's RSS growth / the walsenders' summed RSS (shared memory counted in
each). Machine at load 30-40; rounds interleaved, two at N = 16.

| N | PostgreSQL 18.6 | engine, base (batch 1, 15f967cd) | engine, this round |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 2.5-2.8 s, CPU 0.28-0.30 s, 17 MB | 10.3-10.6 s, CPU 1.8-1.9 s, +16-44 MB | 2.7 s, CPU 0.74 s, +52 MB |
| 4 | 2.5-3.4 s, CPU 1.1 s, 68 MB | 10.9 s, CPU 6.1-6.3 s, +25 MB | 4.6 s, CPU 2.9 s, +52 MB |
| 16 | 7.7-8.1 s, CPU 4.7-5.3 s, 271-274 MB | 12.4-13.2 s, CPU 17-19 s, +50-76 MB | 6.7-10.2 s, CPU 9.4-10.2 s, +55-58 MB |

- **Wall time** was the walsender's keepalive: a subscriber confirms a position past what it
  had to apply (its flush = the received end, when no transaction is pending) only once a
  keepalive tells it where the WAL ends, and the engine sent one every 10 s.
  WalSndWaitForWal sends one as soon as the walsender has caught up and the client hasn't
  reported what was sent; the engine now does too (slots and pg_stat_replication's lag follow
  at once).
- **CPU**: the walsender wrote (a syscall) after every message; it now writes every 64 kB and at
  each transaction's end (half the CPU). What is left is each walsender decoding the same
  transactions on its own (`decode::changes` and pgoutput per sender, linear in N: 0.6 s per
  subscriber against PostgreSQL's 0.3 s). Sharing it, a per-database cache of decoded
  transactions (`Rec`s, keyed by commit position, kept while a slot behind needs it) and the
  pgoutput bytes of senders with the same options and publications, is the next step.
- **Memory**: one process's growth against PostgreSQL's per-process walsenders (16 MB each).
  (The "before" RSS of the two engine builds differs, 120-140 MB / 370 MB, before any
  walsender starts: the base binary came from `cargo test --release`'s feature set, this one
  from `cargo build --release`; compare the growth.)
- The commit path is unchanged (walsender code only).

## Catalog round 7: catalog rows by column (round 22, agent 115)

Agent 92's catalogs (`scale --keep 1 --schemas 20`: 10,000 tables and a table of 1,000
partitions; `scale --tables 25000 --parts 10000` then `scale-more`: about 90,000 objects),
release builds, a fresh copy of the data directory per run, the engine on cores 20-23, psql in
a container; PostgreSQL 18.6 in `a115-bpg` / `a115-bpg9` (`run.sh pg-start` settings). **base**
is `4025ec2b`, **new** this batch. The machine was loaded throughout (load 25-55 from other
agents' builds and suites): runs interleaved, three rounds at 10,000 tables, two at 90,000,
medians; the engine's CPU is the process's user + system time.

### Memory (engine RSS; PostgreSQL: shared buffers holding catalog pages)

| | PostgreSQL | base | new |
|---|---|---|---|
| 10,000 tables: database open | | 108 MB | 99 MB |
| 10,000 tables: pg_attribute, pg_type, pg_class read | 36 MB of buffers, 2.5 MB a backend (100 MB of catalogs on disk) | 402 MB | 133 MB |
| 10,000 tables: peak | | 404 MB | 149 MB |
| 90,000 objects: database open | | 286 MB | 250 MB |
| 90,000 objects: the three catalogs read | 120 MB of buffers (314 MB on disk) | 1,173 MB | 330 MB |
| 90,000 objects: peak | | 1,191 MB | 410 MB |
| engine CPU for the run (10,000 / 90,000) | | 0.98 / 2.6 s | 0.74 / 2.2 s |
| catalog heap after open, 10,000 tables (counting allocator) | | 58.4 MB | 47.9 MB |

(The engine's figures include its 25 MB at start: binary, built-in catalogs.) What was heavy:
generated rows as `Vec<Value>`, 32 bytes a column (pg_attribute's 25 columns: 800 bytes a row,
and a string allocated per name), all of a catalog's rows held before they were chunked
(the peak), and `relindex`'s one-member multisets (a `PMap` each, six allocations). Rows are
now kept by column in chunks of about 64 (one value, a plain vector, or a dictionary per
column), chunked as they are generated, and a multiset of one member is inline.

### Catalog queries (ms, psql over the wire; first in a new session, then again)

| query | PostgreSQL | base | new |
|---|---|---|---|
| 10,000 tables: `sum(attnum)` of pg_attribute | 60 / 24 | 381 / 108 | 219 / 62 |
| `count(typname)` of pg_type | 8.4 / 3.7 | 95 / 15 | 100 / 9.8 |
| `count(relname)` of pg_class | 13.8 / 6.6 | 113 / 29 | 111 / 20 |
| 90,000 objects: pg_attribute | 77 / 57 | 966 / 244 | 699 / 156 |
| pg_type | 25 / 14.5 | 263 / 54 | 310 / 36 |
| pg_class | 38 / 20 | 314 / 83 | 271 / 56 |

pg_type's first read at 90,000 objects is slower (it is still generated whole and then
chunked: its rows are changed in place once all are made); every other first read and every
later one is faster, since rows are made per scan only with the columns a plan reads.

`catalog-tools` at 10,000 tables (ms per query after the first, medians of 2 interleaved
rounds; the whole session's engine CPU 2.55 s → 1.85 s, RSS 272 → 155 MB):

| query | PostgreSQL | base | new |
|---|---|---|---|
| psql `\dt` | 15.0 | 23.8 | 23.0 |
| psql `\d t` indexes | 1.4 | 13.3 | 6.4 |
| Django's table list | 19.2 | 46.9 | 26.3 |
| Prisma's columns of a schema | 138 | 183 | 102 |
| pgAdmin's table list | 64.9 | 32.5 | 23.9 |
| the ORM constraints query | 49.0 | 21.1 | 19.4 |

`\d`'s pg_constraint scan (`contype IN ('p','u','x') AND conrelid = x`, its index condition
derived from the join) read every constraint: the index path's equality is now looked up as
the filter wrote it too, and a filter's equality after conjuncts that can't raise anything
picks the rows. Django's `relkind IN (...) AND pg_table_is_visible(oid)` ran `relkind`'s test
through `eval` on every pg_class row (the filter didn't compile as a whole): its compiled
prefix now decides first. Prisma's per-column lookups of one relation's pg_attribute rows
walked the built-in relations' rows each time.

### pgbench

Scale 10, 4 clients, 10,000 transactions each, three interleaved rounds on the loaded machine
(tps varied 2x between rounds; the engine's CPU per transaction is the steadier measure):

| | base | new |
|---|---|---|
| `-S` tps (median) | 5,872 | 6,177 |
| `-S` engine CPU per 1,000 transactions | 312 ms | 311 ms |
| `-N` tps (median) | 1,171 | 1,882 |
| `-N` engine CPU per 1,000 transactions | 1,032 ms | 916 ms |

Unchanged within the noise, as expected: no plan of a user table reads generated catalog rows.

### Verification (round 22, agent 115, first batch)

`cargo test -p pg-engine` debug and release (every test binary, deep_input, catalog_format,
catalog_storage and ddl_scaling with them), clippy (no new warnings), `difftest smoke`; the
isolation suite on a private reference: 121 / 122 specs, 1,647 / 1,648 permutations, and
`roll-concurrent-create`; the regression suite in full (debug build, private reference
`a115-ref1`) against the committed `results.tsv`: 51,148 / 51,150, no unit changed;
`pg_dump_tap` 14,817 / 14,817; `pg_dump_roundtrip` over that run's database: 0 errors restoring
either side's dump into the other, every way.

### Second batch: restoring PostgreSQL's dump of the regression database

The regression suite's database on a private PostgreSQL 18.6 (`PGREGRESS_KEEP=1`), dumped with
`pg_dump` (12.9 MB plain, 4,099 statements; the globals restored first), restored into a fresh
database with psql (`-e`, `\timing`, each statement timed) or with pg_restore from a custom
dump; release builds:

| restore | PostgreSQL | base | new |
|---|---|---|---|
| psql of the plain dump (wall) | 4.2 s | 7.0 s | 7.0 s |
| pg_restore of the custom dump (wall) | | | 8.5 s |
| engine CPU, peak RSS (psql) | | 7.0 s, 209 MB | 7.3 s, 223 MB |

`pg_dump_roundtrip`'s 35-90 s for these restores are its debug-built engine's (85 s for the same
pg_restore). By statement kind (psql; seconds, PostgreSQL / new):

| | PostgreSQL | new |
|---|---|---|
| COPY (637) | 0.94 | 2.2-2.6 |
| CREATE INDEX (106) | 0.78 | 1.85 |
| CREATE UNIQUE INDEX (42) | 0.07 | 0.33 (0.57 before) |
| CREATE TABLE (721) | 0.88 | 0.37 |
| ALTER TABLE ... OWNER TO (724) | 0.18 | 0.26 |
| ALTER TABLE ... ADD CONSTRAINT ... PRIMARY KEY (124) | 0.17 | 0.15 |

Expression and partial index builds restricted the search path and pushed the table's owner
per row (`index_values`); one security-restricted operation per build now, as PostgreSQL's
index_build: `CREATE INDEX ON t ((seqno + 1))` over 10,000 rows 185 → 97 ms (PostgreSQL 8),
the regression database's `hash_f8_index_3` 225 → 87 ms. What remains is the bulk write path:
COPY (quad_poly_tbl 229 ms against 103, agg_data_20k 74 against 14, tenk2 80 against 34) and
plain btree builds (tenk1_unique1 57 against 6.4 ms: memtable skiplist inserts, the
transaction's spill map, SST writes), for agent 106's round.

Kysely's suite (agent 100's end-to-end measure of catalog reads after DDL: 745 tests, the engine
fresh per run, two interleaved rounds): engine CPU 11.8 / 11.9 s for base, 12.9 / 10.9 s for
new, peak RSS 106-108 / 104-107 MB: unchanged within the noise (agent 100 measured PostgreSQL
at 7.6 s). Its catalogs after each DDL statement were spliced before this round too; what it
pays is regeneration of the catalogs that aren't (pg_depend, information_schema) and the
planning of its catalog joins.

### Verification (round 22, agent 115, second batch)

Both batches rebased onto `3f56fe3f` (agent 100's information_schema views, now kept as shared
rows, go through the chunked generation too): `cargo test -p pg-engine` debug and release (on
the rebase before it, `wire_waits`' `waits_end_by_timeouts_and_cancel` failed once under load
with "error communicating with the server" in place of "connection closed", the
transaction_timeout flake agent 109 describes, and passed when run again), clippy (no new
warnings), `difftest smoke`; isolation 121 / 122 specs, 1,647 / 1,648 permutations, and
`roll-concurrent-create`; the regression suite in full (debug, private reference
`a115-ref3`): 51,148 / 51,150, no unit changed; `pg_dump_tap` 14,817 / 14,817;
`pg_dump_roundtrip`: 0 errors restoring the engine's dump into PostgreSQL every way, and
PostgreSQL's into the engine every way but one run of `pg_restore -j 4` (1 error, below).
Then rebased onto `898ef33a` and `629f3e51` (no file of these batches changed upstream but
the docs): crate tests debug and release again (`crash_async_commit`'s
`kill_during_async_commits`, new upstream, failed once parsing a log line cut short by the
kill, and passed 5 of 5 runs alone), clippy.

`pg_restore -j 4` of PostgreSQL's regression database into the engine fails one statement in
about one run in ten, before these batches too: `ALTER TABLE ONLY ... ADD CONSTRAINT ...
FOREIGN KEY` raises `relation "<oid>" does not exist` (`Ddl::note_lock`: the oid it locks
isn't in the command's catalog copy), and the constraint's COMMENT then fails with it
(`clstr_tst_con`, `dropfk_a_fkey`, `comment_test_child_fk`, ...). Release builds, a fresh
engine per run: `4025ec2b` 6 of 54 runs, `b3568d3e` 1 of 60, these batches 9 of 193 (the
same rate). The concurrent DDL path is agent 109's area; it was not looked into further here.

## Memory under load (round 22, agent 125)

HANDOFF.md, "Memory under load", has what changed. Release builds over the wire on one
machine shared with other agents' builds (load average 25-30, so CPU figures vary by about
10%); `base` is d6746f74 (this round's start), `new` this round's first batch. PostgreSQL
18.6 is the pinned image with its defaults (`shared_buffers` 128 MB, `work_mem` 4 MB) and
`max_connections=300`; its memory is the container's anon + shmem, the engine's its RSS.
Workload `bench burst` (`tools/bench/burst.rs`) at `burst-load --scale 2`: agent 111's mix
round-robin per session (a PL/pgSQL loop of 10 primary-key lookups, `sum()` of 100 calls of a
SQL function over a FROM, a four-table 36-column join by a random customer, psql's `\d`
column query and an information_schema.columns query, pgbench's TPC-B) for 20 s. The engine
starts afresh for each size (block cache 64 MB, the server's default), so the burst's first
statements open the database: that is the case the 4-8 GB peaks came from.

### Peak and after (MB; `after`: 60 s after the burst with the sessions idle; `closed`: 5 s after they disconnect)

| sessions | | ops/s | CPU µs/op | connected | peak | after | closed |
|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 10 | PostgreSQL | 2,912 | 2,037 | 114 | 138 | 132 | 104 |
| | base | 1,222 | 3,040 | 791 | 1,197 | 569 | 556 |
| | new | 1,574 | 2,600 | 158 | 375 | 356 | 351 |
| 50 | PostgreSQL | 1,777 | 2,339 | 174 | 253 | 245 | 105 |
| | base | 844 | 5,678 | 1,130 | 3,654 | 591 | 580 |
| | new | 1,102 | 2,914 | 179 | 477 | 425 | 394 |
| 100 | PostgreSQL | 4,817 | 2,198 | 245 | 412 | 388 | 108 |
| | base | 886 | 6,642 | 1,665 | 3,172 | 650 | 628 |
| | new | 1,116 | 2,880 | 215 | 593 | 491 | 424 |
| 200 | PostgreSQL | 3,047 | 2,351 | 389 | 686 | 669 | 110 |
| | base | 63 | 75,315 | 2,483 | 4,697 | 701 | 660 |
| | new | 1,143 | 3,063 | 243 | 760 | 603 | 485 |

- **Where base's peak went** (sampling heap profile, `--features heapprof`, 200 sessions, 9.0
  GB live at the peak): 89% in `sizing::ensure`, every session laying out its own heap and
  btree models of pgbench_accounts (200,000 rows) and b_item: the rows read, their layouts and
  index keys, all but one copy thrown away. At 200 sessions the builds also starved the CPU
  (63 operations a second). `new` builds each model once (peak at 200 sessions 4.7 GB -> 760
  MB, 18x the operations).
- **The parse tree cache** held 297 MB of trees (of the 490 MB live after a warm 200-session
  run) under its "32 MB" bound: it counted 8 bytes per byte of text, a tenth of what the trees
  take. It holds 32 MB now (`tests/memory_load.rs`: 33.5 MB freed by emptying it, 32.0 MB
  counted).
- **After the burst**: `new` at 100 sessions (in another run) 579 MB at the end, 431 MB once
  the sessions closed, 434 MB until the database closed (60 s idle), then 190 MB; `base` 717
  MB closed and 634 MB three minutes later, the database closed too: nothing ran the
  allocator's collection, so the freed memory stayed. What `new` keeps with the database open
  is mostly live (264 MB live of 506 MB RSS 55 s after a 100-session burst: the size models
  ~130 MB, the block cache 64 MB, the parse trees 32 MB) plus mimalloc's partly used pages.
  PostgreSQL's backends keep their memory until they disconnect, then it is gone (104-110
  MB with shared buffers).
- mimalloc's purge delay (`MIMALLOC_PURGE_DELAY`, default 1,000 ms): 10 ms gave a 531 MB
  peak at 100 sessions (593), 0 ms 488 MB, at 3-9% more CPU per operation; left at the
  default.
- **Not fixed: per-statement working memory.** The mix with `--mix sort,agg` added (a sort of
  20,000 rows under a LIMIT, a grouping of all 200,000 accounts into 50,000 groups) at 100
  sessions: the engine peaked at 5,274 MB and 53 ops/s, PostgreSQL at 733 MB and 902 ops/s.
  The profile (sort and grouping only, 5.2 GB peak): 30% a sequential scan collecting the
  table's rows to return them in heap order (`scan.rs` `next_raw`: 200,000 rows of a table
  whose updates moved rows, about 13 MB per session; agent 117's scan path), 22% the grouping's
  states (`agg.rs` `states`), 22% its keys (each group's key twice: `groups` and `index`),
  7% the hashed order's lookups (`pghash::GroupOrder`, 16 bytes per input row), 4% the hash
  table's growth. PostgreSQL's HashAggregate stays within hash_mem (8 MB) and spills; the
  engine keeps every group (about 45 MB per session here). Spilling where PostgreSQL spills
  (and its EXPLAIN ANALYZE lines, which the engine models already) is the next step.

### Other workloads, base -> new

| | base | new |
|---|---:|---:|
| pgbench -S, 8 clients, prepared: CPU µs / transaction (3 alternated runs) | 112, 119, 119 | 108, 114, 113 |
| pgbench TPC-B, 8 clients, prepared: CPU µs / transaction | 861, 903, 934 | 829, 900, 904 |
| RSS after those runs (MB) | 316-341 | 151-188 |
| TPC-H SF 0.2, 22 queries, sum of medians of 3 (s) | 33.2 | 31.9 |
| TPC-H SF 0.2: server CPU (s), peak RSS (MB) | 57.1, 648 | 56.8, 518 |

The test `tests/memory_load.rs` `many_sessions_stay_bounded` runs 100 sessions of a
smaller mix on a database just opened, in-process with a counting allocator: +80-90 MB of
heap at the peak (release and debug), +640 MB with every session building its own models; it
fails over 40 MB + 0.8 MB a session (`MEMLOAD_SESSIONS`, `MEMLOAD_SECS` for bigger bursts).

## OLTP round 2 (round 22, agent 123)

HANDOFF.md, "OLTP round 2", has what changed: statement templates and kept plans for simple
queries, fast-path relation locks. Release builds with frame pointers, pgbench in a container
on the host network, scale 10, 15 s per run after a 4 s warm-up, the engine restarted per
binary; servers on cores 24–27, pgbench on 28–31; PostgreSQL 18.6 on the host network
(shared_buffers 2 GB). The machine ran other agents' builds and suites throughout (load
15–35, every core 75–100% busy), so CPU time moves a lot between runs; instructions per
transaction (`perf stat` of the engine process, PostgreSQL's cgroup) move less, but include
the engine's background flushes and compactions, which explains most single-run outliers.
**base** is `629f3e51`, **new** this round on it; one interleaved run each (CPU ms /
kinstr per transaction, peak memory: the engine's VmHWM, PostgreSQL's memory.peak).

**Simple protocol** (`-M simple`, pgbench's default; ORMs' unnamed statements are next):

| workload | clients | base | new | PostgreSQL | new peak (base) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| tpcb-like | 1 | 1.44 ms / 1,765 | 0.87 / 1,572 | 0.73 / 1,013 | 281 MB (517) |
| tpcb-like | 8 | 1.36 / 1,720 | 0.73 / 1,176 | 0.60 / 1,033 | 317 MB (571) |
| tpcb-like | 32 | 1.28 / 2,054 | 0.93 / 1,327 | 0.72 / 1,092 | 341 MB (648) |
| tpcb-like | 64 | 1.57 / 2,201 | 0.98 / 1,434 | 0.84 / 1,174 | 417 MB (618) |
| web-app mix | 1 | 1.91 / 1,976 | 1.07 / 1,239 | 0.89 / 1,182 | 241 MB (464) |
| web-app mix | 8 | 1.76 / 2,417 | 1.11 / 1,566 | 0.79 / 1,183 | 191 MB (917) |
| web-app mix | 32 | 1.76 / 2,362 | 1.16 / 1,692 | 0.74 / 1,182 | 227 MB (532) |
| web-app mix | 64 | 1.79 / 2,440 | 1.40 / 1,895 | 0.89 / 1,193 | 258 MB (705) |

tpcb-like over the simple protocol now costs the engine about what it costs over prepared
statements (0.6–0.9 ms) and 1.2–1.5x PostgreSQL's CPU (was 2–2.3x). Its five statements are
all templated and all keep plans (the accounts / tellers / branches UPDATEs, the SELECT by
key, the history INSERT); BEGIN / END have no literals. In the web-app mix the
`count(*) ... WHERE aid = $n` (a non-unique column: its estimate depends on the value) is
parsed through its template but planned each time. Peak memory fell with it: the base's
binding of every statement allocates more than the kept plans hold (a few KB per template
per session). A profile of the new engine's simple tpcb-like at 8 clients: no query is bound
the usual way any more; 8% is binding a template's generic statement again after a size the
planner reads changed (the tellers' and branches' modelled heaps change size every few
transactions, as PostgreSQL's would and its planner would read), 6% the parse through the
template (scan, shared-cache lookup, tree copy), 3.5% re-reading the sizes a kept plan
depends on.

**Prepared statements** (`-M prepared`): no change beyond noise, as expected (the kept plans
are for simple queries; the fast path saves the lock table's mutex for about ten relation
locks per tpcb-like transaction). Three interleaved rounds, kinstr per transaction:

| workload | clients | base | new |
|---|---|---|---|
| tpcb-like | 8 | 1,136 / 1,164 / 1,111 | 1,117 / 1,143 / 1,136 |
| tpcb-like | 64 | 1,263 / 1,252 / 1,210 | 1,242 / 1,249 / 1,170 |
| simple-update | 1 | 510 / 1,212 / 1,263 | 878 / 554 / 543 |
| web-app mix | 1 | 1,008 / 1,021 / 2,062 | 1,019 / 1,420 / 1,010 |

(The 1-client rows' spread is the background compactions the whole-process count includes.)
One interleaved matrix run, prepared, CPU ms per transaction base / new / PostgreSQL:
tpcb-like 0.91 / 0.91 / 0.52 at 1 client, 0.91 / 0.90 / 0.49 at 8, 0.98 / 0.77 / 0.64 at 32,
0.82 / 0.88 / 0.67 at 64; select-only 0.089 / 0.067 / 0.060, 0.068 / 0.075 / 0.055, 0.076 /
0.096 / 0.064, 0.098 / 0.090 / 0.078; web-app 0.85 / 0.90 / 0.58, 0.85 / 0.84 / 0.55, 0.96 /
0.85 / 0.53, 1.20 / 1.10 / 0.64. Peak memory within 10% of the base's for every row but
web-app at 64 clients (538 MB against 452: its events table, grown by the runs before).

**Where tpcb-like's CPU goes at 32 clients (prepared)**: the accounts UPDATE's index lookup
and row fetch 24% (agent 121's point lookups), sending 14% (one send per Sync), commit 7%,
row locks 5%, `update_row_rls` 4%, relation locks 3% before the fast path, the size model's
`sizing::updated` 2% (agent 126). What is left of this round's list: the prepared and
PL/pgSQL first five custom plans could use the same check to run the generic binding
(HANDOFF.md, "OLTP round 2"); unnamed extended-protocol statements likewise; the web-app
mix's non-unique `count(*)` and its bigserial INSERT; hot-row lock waits at 32+ clients.

## Replication, round 5 (round 22, agent 129)

HANDOFF.md "Round 5 (agent 129)" has what changed. Release builds; every engine with
`--cache-mb 64` (the default) on a fresh data directory, pinned with `taskset`; PostgreSQL
18.6 (the pinned image) in containers on the host network with `--cpuset-cpus`; machine
load 20-45 from other agents. Scripts were in `~/.cache/a129/bench` (apply.sh, pubn.sh,
pgb.sh, e2e.sh; gone with the cache), described here so they can be rebuilt.

**Apply** (`apply.sh`; agent 119's benchmark gave both subscriptions the slot `asub`, so its
PostgreSQL rounds never ran: each subscription now has a slot of its own, made before the
backlog). A publisher holds 60,000 pgbench-like transactions (4 clients: UPDATE of a
10,000-row table by key and INSERT into an indexed history table; one in three also DELETEs
the history's oldest row); each subscriber, created disabled with copy_data = false on its
own slot, is enabled alone and timed until its slot confirmed the end. Default
synchronous_commit of a subscription (off). CPU: the engine process, PostgreSQL's whole
container (apply worker, walwriter, checkpointer). Rounds interleaved, the order rotating.
All subscribers end identical to the publisher (md5 of every row of both tables).

| publisher | subscriber | time | CPU | memory |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| PostgreSQL 18.6 | PostgreSQL 18.6 | 2.8-3.1 s | 3.1-3.3 s | apply worker 33 MB RSS |
| PostgreSQL 18.6 | engine, base ff187f1f | 4.7-5.4 s | 4.3-4.9 s | process +49-52 MB |
| PostgreSQL 18.6 | engine, this round | 4.6-5.1 s | 3.9-4.6 s | process +44-52 MB |
| engine | PostgreSQL 18.6 | 4.3-5.4 s | 4.3-5.3 s | apply worker 34 MB RSS |
| engine | engine, base | 7.0 s | 5.7-6.8 s | process +38-81 MB |
| engine | engine, this round | 7.5-8.1 s | 6.4-6.7 s | process +38-68 MB |

(Load 22 for the PostgreSQL-publisher rows, 20-40 for the engine-publisher ones; in one
round of each engine build the RSS fell, the previous round's memtable going.)
- The apply worker no longer waits for the WAL fsync of each commit: that came with
  b7d5f1fe (its session runs `SET synchronous_commit = off`); agent 119's 15.5 s / 8.6 s CPU
  predates it. Wall time is now about CPU time. What this round changed is the feedback: it
  reports flush only once the commit is durable (`Store::durable_ts`), at no measurable cost.
- The rest of the gap (about 1.3x PostgreSQL's CPU) is the engine's write path (agent 108's
  profile: the size models, memtable searches, allocation), not the apply layer.
- Engine publisher rows: the walsender side costs the engine 0.8-2.1 s CPU against
  PostgreSQL's 0.9-1.5 s; one in three backlog transactions' `DELETE ... WHERE hid = (SELECT
  min(hid) ...)` made the engine's pgbench run take minutes (not replication: the engine's
  min() over a big table with dead versions at its start; left for the planner / storage).

**Fan-out** (`pubn.sh`, agent 119's publisher benchmark): a backlog of 20,000 transactions
(UPDATE by key, INSERT into a history table; 4 clients); N PostgreSQL 18.6 subscriptions of
one publication, each into a database of its own, enabled at once and timed until every
slot confirmed the end. CPU: the engine process / PostgreSQL's walsenders; memory: the
engine's RSS peak over its RSS before / the walsenders' summed RSS peak. Two interleaved
rounds of each.

| N | PostgreSQL 18.6 | engine, base ff187f1f | engine, this round |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1.3-2.1 s, CPU 0.16-0.29 s, 18 MB | 2.3-2.7 s, CPU 0.55-0.81 s, +28-43 MB | 2.5-2.7 s, CPU 0.46 s, +46-50 MB |
| 4 | 1.8-2.1 s, CPU 1.4-1.7 s, 71 MB | 2.8-3.4 s, CPU 2.6-3.0 s, +33-34 MB | 2.9-3.3 s, CPU 0.86-1.06 s, +36-39 MB |
| 16 | 5.5-6.6 s, CPU 4.6-6.9 s, 283 MB | 6.2-6.8 s, CPU 8.4-9.8 s, +36 MB | 6.9-7.2 s, CPU 3.7-3.9 s, +44 MB |

- CPU per walsender went from about 0.55 s per 20,000 transactions to about 0.2 s, under
  PostgreSQL's 0.3-0.4 at N = 4 and 16: decoded once (`decode::Shared`), each relation
  description parsed once (its JSON was a fifth of the profile) and pgoutput's relation
  entries kept across transactions (they were worked out again every transaction).
- Memory: the shared cache's bound is 16 MB of decoded changes per database. The peaks
  above (+28-50 MB over the run, both builds) are mostly the memtable's growth and vary by
  round as much as between the builds; at N = 16 the engine's whole growth is a sixth of
  PostgreSQL's walsenders (17 MB each).
- Wall time is the PostgreSQL subscribers' (both publishers wait for them).

**pgbench check of agent 119's batches** (`pgb.sh`: scale 10, `-M prepared`, 8 clients, 20 s,
`--cache-mb 2048` as tools/bench uses; CPU per transaction of the engine process, VmHWM; 4
rounds interleaved at load 40-45, medians and spread):

| build | select-only CPU / txn | tpcb-like CPU / txn | VmHWM |
|---|---|---|---|
| 898ef33a (before 15f967cd) | 0.100 ms (0.086-0.105) | 0.893 ms (0.825-0.929) | 384 MB (381-392) |
| ff187f1f (agent 119's last) | 0.091 ms (0.084-0.103) | 0.870 ms (0.843-0.952) | 391 MB (390-397) |
| this round | 0.097 ms (0.090-0.101) | 0.904 ms (0.774-0.922) | 385 MB (382-391) |

No regression beyond the rounds' spread (this round's build, which changes nothing on the
OLTP path, moved by as much as the two bases differ). An earlier pair of 3 rounds at load
30-35 agrees (select-only 0.078-0.082 / 0.070-0.089 ms, tpcb-like 0.62-0.78 / 0.74-0.84 ms).

**Side by side** (`e2e.sh`): initial synchronization of four tables (one of 200,000 rows)
under two pgbench clients changing them, then 015_stream.pl-style streamed transactions
(logical_decoding_work_mem = 64kB) with a rolled-back savepoint and an aborted one, REPLICA
IDENTITY FULL updates and a partitioned engine target with partition moves; a PostgreSQL
and an engine subscriber of a PostgreSQL publisher (streaming = on) and of an engine
publisher (streaming = parallel): md5 of every row identical on all three, no
synchronization slot left on the publisher.

## Replication, round 6 (agent 152)

HANDOFF.md "Round 6 (agent 152)" has what changed. Scripts: `tools/bench/repl/` (apply.sh,
pubn.sh, pgb.sh, e2e.sh; README.md there; `PERF=<container>` records a profile of the
engine's cores during the catch-up, `stacks.py` attributes it). Release builds, `--cache-mb
64`, the engine pinned to cores 24-27, PostgreSQL 18.6 (pinned image) on the host network
with `--cpuset-cpus 20-23`, machine load 16-25 from other agents. The engine ran on the host
(a `release` build), in user.slice with every agent's builds, PostgreSQL in its container's
cgroup: the comparisons with PostgreSQL lean against the engine; from 847fbdba on they are
made with both in containers (`run.sh`, `ENGINE_DOCKER=1`) and a `dist` build. Base: `3052087d`
(origin when this batch started, with the round's first commits 2eb31f22 / 90ce701d /
bb338b77); head: this batch.

**Apply** (`apply.sh`, PostgreSQL 18.6 publisher, 60,000 pgbench-like transactions; three
rounds, the subscribers' order rotating; every subscriber ends with the publisher's md5 of
every row):

| subscriber | time | CPU | memory | engine / PostgreSQL CPU |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| PostgreSQL 18.6 | 2.99-3.03 s | 2.38-2.44 s | apply worker 30 MB | |
| engine, base | 4.54-4.78 s | 3.94-4.12 s | process +42-43 MB | 1.66 |
| engine, head | 4.35-4.64 s | 3.84-3.89 s | process +43-49 MB | 1.59 |

Where the engine's apply thread goes (frame-pointer build, `perf record -g`, base): size
models 22% (`exec::sizing::updated` 11.6%, `inserted_with` 10.5%; `btsim::Btree::insert` alone
9.2%: sent to agent 151, whose next pushes cache the last leaf and fold the UPDATE's second
row lookup), commit 17% (memtable inserts 6.5%, WAL append 2.7%, lock release 1.6%), locating
rows 14% (a primary-key row read three times: now once), the per-change statement wrapper 6%,
value input 3% (timestamptz text), the background flush 6.6% of the process. The memory
growth is the store's (memtable and block cache), PostgreSQL's apply worker RSS excludes
its shared buffers.

**Walsenders** (`pubn.sh`: 20,000 transactions, N PostgreSQL subscriptions enabled at once,
timed until each has the publisher's WAL end, then until every slot confirmed it; watched
from the subscriber now, since a query on the engine every 0.1 s, a connection each, was
counted as the walsender's cost):

| N | PostgreSQL 18.6 | engine, head | engine / PostgreSQL time, CPU, memory |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0.95 s, CPU 0.15 s, 16 MB | 0.80-0.99 s, CPU 0.34-0.41 s, +43-46 MB | 0.95, 2.5, 2.8 |
| 4 | 1.51-1.84 s, CPU 0.97-0.99 s, 64 MB | 0.92-1.06 s, CPU 0.82-0.93 s, +51-53 MB | 0.6, 0.9, 0.8 |
| 16 | 4.77-5.05 s, CPU 2.81-3.33 s, 256 MB | 1.66-1.67 s, CPU 2.74-2.77 s, +65-77 MB | 0.34, 0.9, 0.3 |

(Engine CPU is the whole process; PostgreSQL's the walsenders'. Base, same runs at N = 1 / 4:
0.31-0.56 s / 1.07-1.10 s.) One walsender's own work (profile, engine threads only):
decoding 48% (two thirds of it the store scan of the records), the change stream's removal
36% (`log::gc_writes` reads every confirmed record back to write a tombstone for it, once a
second), output 8%. With `--cache-mb 8` the growth at N = 1 is +28-32 MB: about 15 MB of it is
the block cache filled by reading the records back.

**Side by side** (`e2e.sh`, all scenarios: streamed transactions with rolled-back nested
subtransactions, an exception block and DDL in a rolled-back one, streaming = on and
parallel; row filter with column list; publish_via_partition_root with TRUNCATE; two-phase
with a streamed prepare; disable_on_error and SKIP; conflict statistics; run_as_owner and its
change while running; failover slots; origin = none both ways): every check identical on
the PostgreSQL and engine subscribers of both publishers. Base failed `run_as_owner` (the
engine subscriber of the engine publisher applied the next transaction with the old
setting); before the round's first commits, `via_root` (the root's TRUNCATE) and
`twophase` (engine to engine) differed too.

**test_decoding** 1,043 / 1,047 (base 1,043: the TOAST accounting fixes stream's TOAST case
and costs its multi-insert case, HANDOFF).

**pgbench check** (`pgb.sh`: scale 10, `-M prepared`, 8 clients, 20 s, `--cache-mb 2048`;
four rounds interleaved at load 16-25 with other agents' builds; CPU per transaction of the
engine process, VmHWM): select-only base 0.145-0.181 ms, head 0.148-0.168 ms; tpcb-like base
0.759-0.931 ms, head 0.736-0.932 ms; VmHWM base 410-440 MB, head 420-450 MB. No change beyond
the rounds' spread (this batch touches no OLTP path).

**Verified** (this batch): crate tests debug and release (deep_input and logical_apply
included; `run_as_owner_and_privileges` now changes run_as_owner while the worker runs),
clippy without new warnings, test_decoding 1,043 / 1,047, the side-by-side scripts above all
identical, isolation 121 / 122 (intra-grant-inplace, the known L2 difference) and the 17
roll-* specs 17 / 17 on a private reference; a regress subset (subscription, publication,
replica_identity, create_view) on a private reference. The full regress and pg_dump_tap
runs weren't repeated for this batch: it changes the apply worker, the decoder and a
sizing helper only the decoder calls, none of which those suites run.

**test_decoding stats units** (sent to agent 155, planner): three slot-creating function
scans in one FROM are planned as `Nested Loop (Function Scan s3, Materialize (Nested Loop
(s1, s2)))`; PostgreSQL 18.6 nests s1 and s2, then s3, so its slots are created a, b, c and
take those places in pg_replication_slots, the engine's c, a, b:

```sql
EXPLAIN (COSTS OFF) SELECT 'init' FROM pg_create_logical_replication_slot('a', 'test_decoding') s1,
  pg_create_logical_replication_slot('b', 'test_decoding') s2, pg_create_logical_replication_slot('c', 'test_decoding') s3;
-- (with generate_series(1, 1) in their place both nest s1, s2, then s3)
```


## Size models, round 2 (round 22, agent 126)

HANDOFF.md, "Size models, round 2", has what changed. Release builds with frame pointers, the
engine pinned to cores 24-27 and pgbench (`-M prepared`, a container on the host network) to
28-31, scale 10, 20 s per run after a 5 s warm-up, the engine restarted per binary on one
data directory, base (`629f3e51`) and head interleaved. The machine was saturated (load
40-50, every core 94-99% busy), so tps and CPU
per transaction moved by up to 2x between identical runs; context switches per transaction
(`perf stat -e context-switches` of the engine) are what the change is about and held still.

| workload | clients | base tps | head tps | base CPU/txn | head CPU/txn | base cs/txn | head cs/txn | base VmHWM | head VmHWM |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| tpcb-like | 8 | 4,947 / 3,005 | 5,560 / 3,045 | 0.54 / 0.79 ms | 0.53 / 0.73 ms | 14.6 / 15.7 | 13.3 / 13.0 | 305 / 286 MB | 306 / 296 MB |
| tpcb-like | 32 | 5,500 / 1,549 | 4,274 / 2,646 | 0.60 / 1.09 ms | 0.76 / 0.97 ms | 20.7 / 21.0 | 19.7 / 17.9 | 342 / 342 MB | 334 / 333 MB |
| tpcb-like | 64 | 3,311 / 2,135 | 2,863 / 3,092 | 0.76 / 1.12 ms | 0.99 / 1.07 ms | 33.5 / 30.8 | 28.8 / 24.8 | 421 / 390 MB | 439 / 401 MB |
| simple-update | 8 | 7,372 / 4,335 | 6,574 / 8,228 | 0.43 / 0.45 ms | 0.44 / 0.39 ms | 9.5 / 8.7 | 9.1 / 9.3 | 313 / 301 MB | 305 / 329 MB |
| simple-update | 32 | 9,097 / 8,943 | 7,532 / 8,751 | 0.41 / 0.42 ms | 0.46 / 0.44 ms | 8.4 / 8.5 | 8.1 / 8.2 | 351 / 360 MB | 381 / 388 MB |
| simple-update | 64 | 7,952 / 6,892 | 8,472 / 8,379 | 0.47 / 0.50 ms | 0.45 / 0.45 ms | 8.3 / 8.3 | 8.0 / 8.0 | 469 / 454 MB | 543 / 514 MB |

- Off-CPU (`perf record -e sched:sched_switch -g`, 64 clients of tpcb-like, 16 s; the first
  engine frame under each switch): base 650,000 switches at 2,100 tps, of them 245,000 under
  the size models' lock (`ensure` 10.8%, `fetched` 7.3%, `is_estimated` 5.6%, `updated`
  5.2%, `CopyLoad::register` 4.7%, `check_size`, `untracked_write`, `inserted_with`,
  `mark_passed`), about 7 per transaction; head 116,000 at 1,080 tps, 4,400 of them in the
  models (0.25 per transaction). The rest are the client's round trips, the WAL fsync wait
  and the row locks on pgbench_branches.
- simple-update writes no modelled table at scale 10 but pgbench_history, so it changes
  little. Its VmHWM at 64 clients was 40-75 MB higher on head in both rounds (allocation by
  more threads at once, no model grows); agent 125's area, to watch.
- Per-write profile after the change (8 clients, `perf record -g -a` on the engine's cores):
  scale 1 9.8% of the engine's samples in the models (`fetched` → `Heap::prune_opt` 2.7%,
  `Kills::mark` → `kill_needed` 1.6%, `updated` 1.9%, `inserted_with` 0.5%), scale 10 5.5%
  (`row_roots` 1.5%, `updated` 1.5%, `inserted_with` 0.7%, `fetched` 0.6%).

**A clean restart against PostgreSQL 18.6** (a mixed workload, then each relation's pages per
fork and two EXPLAINs, a restart, the same again, then inserts before and after a VACUUM):

```
CREATE TABLE mw1 (id int PRIMARY KEY, k int, v text, pad char(40)); CREATE INDEX mw1_k ON mw1 (k);
CREATE INDEX mw1_v ON mw1 (v); CREATE TABLE mw2 (id bigserial PRIMARY KEY, ref int, note text);
CREATE INDEX mw2_ref ON mw2 (ref);
INSERT INTO mw1 SELECT g, g % 1000, md5(g::text), 'p' FROM generate_series(1, 60000) g;
INSERT INTO mw2 (ref, note) SELECT g % 5000, repeat('n', g % 40) FROM generate_series(1, 40000) g;
UPDATE mw1 SET k = k + 1 WHERE id % 7 = 0; UPDATE mw1 SET pad = 'q' WHERE id % 5 = 0;
UPDATE mw1 SET v = md5(v) WHERE id % 11 = 0; DELETE FROM mw1 WHERE id % 13 = 0;
DELETE FROM mw2 WHERE id % 3 = 0; VACUUM mw2;
INSERT INTO mw1 SELECT g, g % 50, md5((g * 3)::text), 'r' FROM generate_series(60001, 70000) g;
INSERT INTO mw2 (ref, note) SELECT g % 700, 'again' FROM generate_series(1, 8000) g;
UPDATE mw2 SET note = note || '!' WHERE ref % 9 = 0; DELETE FROM mw1 WHERE id BETWEEN 20000 AND 26000;
```

| pages (main / fsm / vm) | PostgreSQL, before = after | head, before = after | base before | base after |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| mw1 | 1176 / 3 / 0 | 1176 / 3 / 0 | 1176 / 3 / 0 | 1176 / 0 / 0 |
| mw1_pkey | 357 | 357 | 357 | 166 |
| mw1_k | 100 | 100 | 100 | 53 |
| mw1_v | 725 | 721 | 721 | 434 |
| mw2 | 315 / 3 / 1 | 315 / 3 / 1 | 315 / 3 / 1 | 315 / 0 / 0 |
| mw2_pkey | 134 | 134 | 134 | 97 |
| mw2_ref | 65 | 65 | 65 | 46 |

Inserts after the restart: 300 rows into mw1 at (1175,60)-(1178,91), 300 into mw2 at
(217,43)-(225,116), after VACUUM 500 rows into mw1 at (0,7)-(22,33): head's are PostgreSQL's
(base's went to (314,35) and (0,5)). `mw1_v`'s 721 pages against 725 is the model's before the
restart too (md5 text keys' page splits), not the restart's.

**First statement after a clean restart** (300,000 rows of pgbench_accounts' shape, a tenth
updated; over the wire, psql's `\timing`; two interleaved rounds at load ~40): base 186 / 202
ms, head 172 / 181 ms, VmHWM 145-148 MB both. The database opens on its first connection, so
the background builder is laying out that very table when the first statement asks for it.
What's left of the layout (perf): `heapsim::restored` 59% (the key map's inserts and their
allocations), the record's decoding 23% (`Btree::read_image` 13%), `read_shape` 2%.

**Model memory** (item 5): VmRSS of a freshly opened engine 49.7 MB; after the 300,000-row
table's models are laid out 156.7 MB, about 356 bytes per row with the primary key (1.2 GB per
million rows; PostgreSQL's pages for them: 44 MB heap and 6.8 MB index).

## Catalog round 8 (round 23, agent 131)

Release builds; **base** is `77a38372` (the branch's head when the round began), **new** each
batch. The machine was loaded throughout (load 25-45 from other agents): runs interleaved,
the engine pinned to four cores, its CPU time from `/proc/<pid>/stat`, peak RSS `VmHWM`.

### First batch: `pg_restore -j 4`'s foreign keys, DDL after its locks

PostgreSQL's regression database (a private PostgreSQL 18.6 after a full `pg_regress` run with
`PGREGRESS_KEEP=1`, `pg_dump -Fc`: 3.4 MB, 1,669 relations, 65 foreign keys) restored with
`pg_restore -j 4` into a fresh engine per run:

| | runs | runs failing a statement |
|---|---|---|
| base | 95 | 5 (`relation "21402" does not exist`, ...: one FOREIGN KEY each) |
| new | 200 | 0 |

The oid was an index's: `perms::rel_word` didn't name indexes, and `Ddl::note_lock` took a
relation it couldn't name for one dropped while its lock was waited for whenever another
session's DDL committed between its two catalog reads; an RI check locks both tables'
indexes. Spec `roll-ddl-after-lock-waits` reproduces it every time (an ALTER INDEX waiting for
another ALTER INDEX's lock, which now exists).

| | base | new |
|---|---|---|
| restore wall (15 interleaved runs each, median; a build running alongside) | 5.3 s | 5.2 s |
| pgbench `-S` tps (3 rounds) | 9,672 / 9,014 / 10,449 | 9,634 / 9,278 / 11,264 |
| `-S` engine CPU per 1,000 transactions | 236-248 ms | 251-265 ms |
| pgbench `-N` tps | 2,846 / 3,303 / 3,402 | 2,870 / 3,151 / 3,272 |
| `-N` engine CPU per 1,000 transactions | 703-803 ms | 699-763 ms |
| pgbench peak RSS (`-N`) | 489-519 MB | 494-518 MB |
| Kysely's suite: engine CPU (2 rounds), peak RSS | 11.8 / 11.0 s, 100-103 MB | 11.1 / 10.5 s, 101-103 MB |
| `catalog-tools` at 10,000 tables: engine CPU, RSS | 1.88 / 1.83 s, 170-174 MB | 1.90 / 1.86 s, 172 MB |

(pgbench: scale 10, 4 clients, 10,000 transactions each, `--no-sync`. Unchanged within the
noise, as expected: the batch changes which locks DDL takes and when it reads the catalog
again, not the statements' own work.)

`catalog-tools` at 10,000 tables (ms, first in the session / again; medians of 2 rounds):
unchanged within the noise (psql `\dt` 140 / 23.0 → 148 / 22.3, `\d t` indexes 50 / 4.0 →
51 / 4.2, Django's tables 27.5 / 26.4 → 29.2 / 26.3, Prisma's columns 124 / 104 → 104 / 106,
the ORM constraints query 19.3 / 17.6 → 22.2 / 19.4).

## OLTP round 3 (round 23, agent 136)

HANDOFF.md, "OLTP round 3", has what changed: extended-protocol statements bound once per
text, their custom plans run as the generic binding when that is provably their plan, kept
plans by non-unique columns without a most-common-values list, DML re-planned without
re-binding, kept plans' sizes checked without a statement. Release builds with frame
pointers, pgbench in a container on the host network, scale 10, 10 s per run after a 4 s
warm-up, the engine restarted per binary and round; servers on cores 24–27, pgbench on
28–31; PostgreSQL 18.6 on the host network (shared_buffers 2 GB). **base** is `fd250f0b`,
**new** this round on it; three interleaved rounds (base, new, PostgreSQL), each cell the
range over the rounds: CPU ms per transaction (the engine process; PostgreSQL's cgroup),
in brackets the engine's thousand instructions per transaction (`perf stat` of the
process), then the median tps and the peak memory (the engine's VmHWM over the round's
runs; PostgreSQL's memory.peak, cumulative). The machine ran at load 40–55 throughout (other
agents' builds, suites and benchmarks), so single numbers move a lot; the whole-process
instruction count includes background flushes and compactions, whose timing follows the run
order (new ran after base in every round, so it often paid for base's writes: the
`ckinstr` runs below count only the connection threads).

- **Extended protocol** (pgbench `-M extended`: Parse / Bind / Describe / Execute / Sync of
  the unnamed statement, what pgx, node-postgres, JDBC before prepareThreshold and asyncpg's
  unnamed statements send): 2.3–3.0x less CPU per transaction. Select-only now costs what
  PostgreSQL's does (0.10–0.16 ms against 0.12–0.15); tpcb-like and the web-app mix are within
  10–25% of PostgreSQL (were 2.5–3.5x). The base bound every unnamed statement twice (the
  Parse's generic binding, then the Bind's custom plan). Peak memory unchanged (365 MB against
  381 at tpcb-like; the select rows' 522 against 431 is the web-app events table, which grows
  by every run and is first looked at by the size model in new's runs).
- **Simple protocol**: unchanged beyond noise in this matrix; the profile of tpcb-like at 8
  clients shows binding and planning down from 8.0% to 7.2% of the engine's samples (the
  accounts UPDATE re-planned, not re-bound, when its heap grows: 42% of executions; its
  re-bind's analysis was a smaller part than round 2 thought, plan_query is 3.1%), size
  checks 1.1% (were a statement snapshot each). A direct A/B of the connection threads'
  instructions only (`ckinstr`, three rounds at 8 clients): base 1,075–2,551, new 1,036–2,689
  thousand per transaction; the whole-process count's 1,456–1,597 against 945–1,048 above is
  background work, which ran in new's runs.
- **Web-app mix**: `count(*) ... WHERE aid = $n` keeps its plan (no most-common-values list
  on `aid`); its bigserial INSERT is 6% of the mix's CPU (size model 1.3%, the unique key's
  conflict probe 1.6%, nextval 0.4%), none of it planning.
- **Connection per transaction** (`pgbench -C`, `SELECT 1`, both servers trusting the
  client): the engine 0.55 ms of CPU per connection and 1,170 connections a second from one
  client, 2,073 from eight; PostgreSQL 7.6 ms (its fork and backend start) and 209 / 421. With
  the engine's `--password` (SCRAM) and PostgreSQL's image trusting 127.0.0.1, pgbench
  showed 49 against 134: the client's PBKDF2 (13 ms per connection in pgbench's loaded
  container) against none, measured phase by phase (startup to AuthenticationSASL 0.2 ms,
  SCRAM's server side 0.4 ms, the first ReadyForQuery 0.06 ms).
- **Hot rows** (tpcb-like `-M prepared`, one round): engine / PostgreSQL tps at 1, 8, 32, 64,
  128 clients 1,184 / 1,528, 3,140 / 4,757, 1,478 / 3,068, 1,358 / 3,253, 1,586 / 3,069; CPU
  per transaction 0.67 / 0.45, 0.91 / 0.51, 1.15 / 0.78, 1.11 / 0.92, 1.38 / 1.03 ms. The
  engine runs out of tps before CPU at 32+ (1.7 of its 4 cores busy): off-CPU, about a fifth
  of its threads' context switches are `sched_yield` spinning on the size model's relfiles
  lock (`Kills::new`, `ensure` from `updated`, `fetched`, `is_estimated`, `CopyLoad::register`;
  on-CPU `exec::sizing` is 13.9%), 6.5% the row and xact lock waits, 4% the WAL fsync.
- **Hot rows after the size model's shard locks** (`b0c4238b`, which has agent 142's
  6b079926; two rounds, the machine quieter): engine tps at 1 / 8 / 32 / 64 / 128 clients
  1,612–1,626 / 6,595–6,732 / 6,100–6,698 / 5,194–5,699 / 4,027–4,636, CPU per transaction
  0.49 / 0.47–0.49 / 0.53–0.57 / 0.63–0.64 / 0.78–0.85 ms; PostgreSQL 1,886 / 10,156 / 9,880 /
  8,309 / 7,470 tps, 0.35 / 0.29 / 0.37 / 0.46 / 0.51 ms. The same curve at about two thirds
  of the tps, CPU-bound on the 4 server cores (3.5 busy at 32 clients): the gap is CPU per
  transaction now. Off-CPU at 32: xact-lock waits on the hot rows 8% of the context switches,
  the WAL fsync 5%, `wait_durable`'s sync mutex spinning 4%, the size model's `is_estimated`
  under the branches / tellers bitmap scans 6%, the lock table's mutex under `lock_row` 2%.
  Waking the commits an fsync covers together (a condvar in `wait_durable`) changed neither
  tps nor CPU (1,588–1,732 / 5,826–6,877 / 5,796–6,267 / 4,934–5,593 / 4,272–4,306 tps,
  interleaved with the rows above) and was left out.
- **Agent 133's reproducers**: Metabase's Quartz lock query (`SELECT * FROM qrtz_locks WHERE
  sched_name = .. AND lock_name = .. FOR UPDATE`, its own transaction): 0.107 ms CPU prepared
  and 0.20 simple at 1 client (PostgreSQL 0.136 / 0.154), 16,791 tps at 4 clients on one row
  (PostgreSQL 6,616): the application's 6.7 ms is time waiting for the scheduler transaction
  that holds the row. tpcb-like's END (`pgbench -r -M prepared`, engine `--no-sync`):
  0.060 ms against PostgreSQL's 0.099 with fsync, so a row-writing commit isn't Grafana's
  157 µs average (its migrations' DDL commits are the likely cause, not measured here).

## OLTP, round 4 (round 24, agent 145)

HANDOFF.md, "OLTP round 4", has what changed. Three pushes: the batch of shapes, shared and
watched kept plans and PL/pgSQL custom plans as the generic plan (ef3831c2); a plan's Gathers
found once per bound plan (a6a8b7ec); a custom plan's cost and Gathers from one estimate tree,
the serial tree below parallel_setup_cost, pg_prepared_statements read as the view is
(e8663d91).

### Against PostgreSQL (container on both sides, dist build)

Engine `dist` at e8663d91 in a container of the pinned debian image, PostgreSQL 18.6 in its

round, two interleaved rounds. CPU ms per transaction from /proc of the engine's process and

(other agents' builds): PostgreSQL's own runs moved by up to 2x between rounds (tpcb-like
simple 2,337-5,684 tps), so read the ratios as +-25%.

Peak memory over the matrix: engine VmHWM 425-465 MB; PostgreSQL's cgroup memory.peak 774 MB
(shared_buffers 2 GB, cumulative).

- **select-only**: ahead on every protocol (0.61-0.98). The range read (`SELECT sum(abalance)
  ... WHERE aid BETWEEN :aid AND :aid + 99`) is the gap: 2.3-3.8x the CPU. Its profile
  (release, 8 clients, prepared, after e8663d91): the executor 47% (the aggregate over a
  100-row index scan, agent 149's), the custom plan made on every Execute 36% (re-analysis
  with the values as constants 21%, the serial cost tree 13%). PostgreSQL makes a custom plan
  for every Execute here too (8 of 8, checked), from its analyzed tree: planning the engine's
  `Analyzed` with the values substituted is agent 155's next planner item (the binder decides
  constness while it binds).
- **tpcb-like / simple-update prepared**: 1.0-1.6x. Off-CPU at 32 clients (sched_switch,
  release at f6654266): the size models' locks 26% of the connection threads' context
  switches (agent 151, landing), the WAL fsync 8.6% (fdatasync, and commits spinning on the
  sync mutex while another fsyncs), lock_row 8% (waits for the hot branches / tellers rows
  that PostgreSQL waits for too).

### The fixes, before and after (release, 8 clients, host engine, interleaved)

- **Gathers per bound plan** (a6a8b7ec): since d22006b9 every execution built its plan's
  EXPLAIN tree to start parallel workers when a relation passed min_parallel_table_scan_size
  (40% of select-only's CPU). select-only prepared 0.127-0.137 -> 0.053-0.063 ms per
  transaction, simple 0.139-0.144 -> 0.053-0.064, tpcb-like prepared 0.67-0.87 -> 0.49-0.58.
- **One estimate tree per custom plan, serial below parallel_setup_cost** (e8663d91): the
  range read prepared 0.58 -> 0.36 profile samples per transaction (the Gathers tree gone, the
  cost tree without partial paths); tpcb-like and select-only unchanged (1,419-1,489 /
  133-140 thousand instructions per transaction either side).
- **Commits sleep while another fsyncs** (storage `wait_durable`): simple-update prepared, 8
  clients 0.32-0.40 -> 0.30-0.31 ms per transaction, 32 clients 0.40-0.60 -> 0.31-0.32 ms and
  2,169-8,570 -> 11,978-12,079 tps (PostgreSQL in the container table: 9,159-15,453);
  tpcb-like unchanged.
- **memory_load** (`many_sessions_stay_bounded`, 100 sessions, release): +50.2 MB of heap at
  this round's first batch, +57.0 at d22006b9, +61.7 / +62.4 at 74a3ad38 / b0c4238b (bound
  120); debug builds vary 82-126 MB run to run on any of them.
