| 100-row pk range | extended | 1 | 4,929-5,446 | 2,216-2,548 | 2,986-3,209 | 0.158-0.173 | 0.354-0.413 | 0.281-0.304 | 2.31 | 1.76 |
| 100-row pk range | extended | 8 | 23,260-26,101 | 10,535-11,684 | 12,284-12,920 | 0.148-0.167 | 0.339-0.377 | 0.305-0.321 | 2.27 | 1.98 |
| 100-row pk range | extended | 32 | 20,542-24,161 | 9,254-10,204 | 11,107-12,339 | 0.164-0.193 | 0.391-0.427 | 0.324-0.359 | 2.29 | 1.91 |
| 100-row pk range | prepared | 1 | 6,094-6,292 | 2,543-2,570 | 3,094-3,157 | 0.135-0.139 | 0.355-0.357 | 0.284-0.295 | 2.60 | 2.11 |
| 100-row pk range | prepared | 8 | 29,066-32,323 | 10,412-10,879 | 13,403-14,425 | 0.118-0.132 | 0.365-0.381 | 0.274-0.296 | 2.98 | 2.28 |
| 100-row pk range | prepared | 32 | 19,623-31,403 | 10,416-10,612 | 9,204-12,282 | 0.126-0.201 | 0.377-0.383 | 0.325-0.429 | 2.33 | 2.31 |

- **The range read** (`SELECT sum(abalance) ... WHERE aid BETWEEN :aid AND :aid + 99`): 15-28%
  less CPU per transaction than base on every protocol (prepared and extended: custom plans
  from the analysis; simple: the template's analysis). Its profile after (release with frame
  pointers, 8 clients, prepared): execution 41% (the aggregate over a 100-row index scan),
  `bound_custom` 19% of which planning 13% (`plan_query_limited`) and the substitution 4.6%,
  the custom plan's cost (`plan_cost`, the EXPLAIN builder's estimate tree) 19.7%: that one
  waits for the planner to hand over its chosen path's cost (agent 155's item).
- **tpcb-like prepared** 1.33-1.59 -> 1.11-1.40; **simple-update prepared** 1.00-1.09 ->
  0.94-1.15 (its 1-client cell rerun in three more interleaved rounds: base 0.244-0.258, new
  0.250-0.254 ms, the same). Where tpcb-like prepared's CPU goes (8 clients): the UPDATEs'
  row scans 31.6% (`next_heap_ordered` 14.8%: tellers' and branches' version chains in their
  hot ranges, which PostgreSQL seq-scans too; storage, agent 149), send 12-14%, receive 6%,
  commit 7.2% (`wait_durable` 2.7%), `run_query`'s own work 8.3% (relation locks 18% of it,
  privilege checks 15%, `begin_statement` 12%, search_path copies 7-10%, settings by name 6%),
  Bind 3%, the allocator 10% as leaf. simple-update prepared: send 18%, commit 12%, receive
  7.6%, `run_query`'s own work 16.5%, pg_stat_activity updates per message 3.5%, the notify
  hub's mutex 1.7% (now per session).
- **select-only** 0.52-1.00 on every protocol (base 0.60-1.11); **tpcb-like simple / extended**
  0.69-0.94 (base 0.80-1.09).
- Peak memory over the matrix: engine VmHWM base 451 MB, new 445 MB; PostgreSQL's cgroup
  memory.peak 1,211 MB (shared_buffers 2 GB, cumulative).

- **Footprint gate** (scoreboard.sh's three measures, the engine in its container on cores 4-7,
  base and this round's measured build alternated, six runs each): fresh data directory 3.0
  MB either side; idle CPU 0-0.05 s per 5 minutes either side; with 100 idle sessions the
  container's anon + shmem 29.3 MB base, 30.7 MB the measured build (RSS 52.4-54.6 against
  55.2-56.4 MB; without sessions 24.7-25.9 against 26.1-26.4 MB). Both pass the gate (33 MB,
  and below PostgreSQL's); the growth was traced to presizing the lock table (`Txn::locks_held`
  with room for 8: a 672-byte allocation class each connection thread's mimalloc heap then
  touches), which was left out of the commit. The committed code wasn't measured again in
  dist (the heavy queue held 20+ jobs): the next scoreboard run should confirm 29-30 MB.

## Application CPU (round 24, agent 133)

Server CPU of the application suites (tests/clients, STATUS.md "Round 7"), profiled with perf
(release engine, line tables and frame pointers, `--call-graph fp`, 199–499 Hz, from a
privileged container on the host's pid namespace) and per statement with pg_stat_statements
on both sides (track = all; the reference with track_planning on, so its planning time counts
as the engine's does). Folded stacks were grouped by phase.

Where the engine's CPU goes (share of samples, base 177b53e9):

| phase | Keycloak | Kysely | EF Core (first 180 s) | owner |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| Parse message: binding and planning the statement (`bound_as`) | 9.6% | 14.6% | 25.6% | planner (140) |
| Bind: the custom plan bound afresh (`bound_custom`) | 15.1% | 9.7% | 1.9% | planner (140) |
| of which path search (`plan_query_limited`) | 13.4% | 14.2% | 23.4% | planner (140) |
| pg_stat_statements' parallel-worker count, an EXPLAIN tree per execution | 12.4% | | | fixed here |
| custom plan cost (`plan_cost_estimate`, an EXPLAIN tree per custom plan) | 6.8% | 6.6% | 1.5% | fixed here, mostly |
| pg_stat_statements' query jumble (the parse tree through serde_json) | 5.4% | | | fixed here |
| Incremental Sort's presorted keys, an EXPLAIN tree per Sort at run time | 6.1% | 3.3% | 3.1% | executor (121) |
| correlated sub-selects run (`eval_subquery`), mostly bitmap scans | 7.9% | | 35.8% | scans (130) |
| commit (`finish_txn`) | 0.5% | 9.1% | 0.9% | OLTP (123) / catalog (131) |

(The Keycloak and Kysely columns ran with pg_stat_statements loaded; without it its rows are 0.)

Fixed here:
- `paths::parallel::may_gather`: pg_stat_statements counts each execution's parallel workers
  from the plan's EXPLAIN tree (`explain::gather_workers`); the tree is built now only when
  some relation the statement reads could get a partial path (min_parallel_table_scan_size,
  a parallel_workers option, an appendrel member, a UNION ALL / inheritance Append, or
  debug_parallel_query). Debug builds assert the tree's count is 0 otherwise.
- `PlanUses::first_custom`: the cost of a prepared statement's first custom plan (read only
  from the sixth execution on, against the generic plan's) is found when its second custom
  plan is made, not as the first runs: a driver's unnamed statement, parsed again for every
  execution (pgjdbc below prepareThreshold, Go's database/sql, Npgsql), runs once and never
  needs it. One custom plan is kept per prepared statement until then (after DDL in between,
  the second plan's cost stands in for the first's). Measured before OLTP round 3 (agent
  136), which runs an unnamed statement's value-independent custom plans as its generic
  binding with their costs likewise deferred (`PlanUses::uncosted`); statements whose text
  it keeps bindings for are costed as their plans are made, as it needs.
- `session::pgss::jumble_cached`: top-level statements' query ids, kept per thread by text,
  position, catalog and search_path (512 entries), as PostgreSQL computes them at parse
  analysis rather than per execution.

Before and after (release, fresh engines, two interleaved runs each; with and without
pg_stat_statements loaded):

| suite | before | after | PostgreSQL |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Kysely | 10.2 / 10.1 s; pgss 11.2 / 12.0 s | 9.2 / 9.7 s; pgss 9.3 / 9.5 s | 7.6 s (pgss) |
| Grafana | 5.0 / 4.8 s; pgss 6.2 / 5.6 s | 4.0 / 4.3 s; pgss 4.7 / 4.3 s | 4.1 s (pgss) |
| Keycloak | 17.5 / 18.2 s; pgss 21.4 / 20.8 s | 17.4 / 16.6 s; pgss 18.2 / 18.4 s | 6.9–9.5 s (pgss) |
| Metabase | 37.9 s | 36.2 s | 13.2 s |

Peak RSS of those runs 98–125 MB (Metabase 174–178 MB) either way (the kept first custom
plans: 0–4 MB more).
pgbench (scale 10, 8 clients, `-M prepared`, 20 s, interleaved twice): select-only 0.115 /
0.116 ms of server CPU per transaction before, 0.110 / 0.110 after (peak 346 / 350 MB
before, 337 / 347 after); tpcb-like 0.755 / 0.768 before, 0.752 / 0.770 after.

EF Core: 919 s of engine CPU (peak 763 MB; 935 s and 841 MB with pg_stat_statements) against
172 s on PostgreSQL. Metabase's gap is mostly Liquibase's column check below (49 calls,
110 ms each) and its own application queries (`SELECT * FROM (SELECT "t"."active", ...`:
~10 ms against ~0.15 ms a call; not profiled further). Most of EF Core's gap is four Northwind
queries, about 530 s of the engine's 805 s of statement time (PostgreSQL: under 0.5 s each),
for the scans' and executor's owners:
- `char(n)` equality index conditions read every entry: on a 20,000-row table,
  `SELECT count(*) FROM t WHERE c = 'ZZZZZ'` (c char(5), btree) is an Index Only Scan with
  "Heap Fetches: 20000" and 0 rows, 84–96 ms (PostgreSQL 0.2 ms, 2 buffers). Northwind's
  CustomerID is char(5): every correlated `o0."CustomerID" = c."CustomerID"` sub-select (a
  Bitmap Heap Scan per outer row) reads all 830 orders, ~3.6 ms a run (PostgreSQL ~10 µs).
  `int` keys don't (0.8 ms for none), but 5,000 matching int rows still take 46 ms
  (PostgreSQL 1.3 ms).
- A sub-select's InitPlans that read only the outer query's row (`(SELECT o1."OrderID" ...
  WHERE c."CustomerID" = o1."CustomerID" ORDER BY ... LIMIT 1) = o."OrderID"` inside a
  correlated sub-select) are evaluated per row of the sub-select's own scan, not once per
  run: "Order Details" (2,155 rows) times two InitPlans per customer, 92 s for 8 customers
  where the plan (the same as PostgreSQL's) says Index Only Scan with `Index Cond:
  ("OrderID" = (InitPlan 2).col1)`.

For the planner's owner: Liquibase's column check (Keycloak, Metabase), `SELECT 1 FROM
pg_attribute a WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM pg_class JOIN pg_catalog.pg_namespace ns ON ns.oid
= pg_class.relnamespace WHERE lower(ns.nspname) = 'public' AND lower(relname) = 't77' AND
pg_class.oid = a.attrelid) AND lower(a.attname) = 'c5'`, isn't pulled up to a semi join
(PostgreSQL: a HashAggregate of the pg_class ⋈ pg_namespace side, then an index scan of
pg_attribute); the engine runs a SubPlan per pg_attribute row (6,401 loops): 74–110 ms a call
against 1 ms. Any qual on the namespace side keeps it a SubPlan (without one it is pulled
up), and the SubPlan's EXPLAIN shows `Recheck Cond: (oid = ns.oid)`, an equivalence the query
doesn't have (the rows are right). pgjdbc's getTables / getImportedKeys / getIndexInfo cost
5–40x PostgreSQL's per call (Keycloak's Liquibase calls getTables 991 times). For OLTP:
Grafana's 1,430 COMMITs average 157 µs against 1 µs (`--no-sync`), and Metabase's `SELECT *
FROM QRTZ_LOCKS ... FOR UPDATE` 6.7 ms against 0.4 ms.
## Query memory and spilling (round 23, agent 132)

HANDOFF.md, "Query memory and spilling", has what changed: sorts and hash aggregations
honour work_mem / hash_mem_multiplier and spill where PostgreSQL's would (decided by
PostgreSQL's own count of their memory), group keys are kept once and group states are
compact. Release builds over the wire, server pinned to 4 cores, on a machine shared with
other agents (load 30-40 in the first runs; everything at nice 19 in the later ones), engine
memory its RSS, PostgreSQL's the container's anon + shmem. PostgreSQL 18.6 is the pinned image
with its defaults (`work_mem` 4 MB, `shared_buffers` 128 MB) and `max_connections=300`.

### Burst, the sort + grouping mix (`bench burst --mix pl,fn,orm,cat,tpcb,sort,agg`, scale 2)

Agent 111's mix plus a 20,000-row sort under a LIMIT and a 50,000-group grouping of the
200,000 accounts, 20 s per size, a fresh engine per size. `base` is 57d7da04, `new` this batch
(on fd250f0b; the two differ also by agent 126's size-model shards, which don't touch this
mix's peak), run interleaved per size. MB.

| sessions | PostgreSQL peak | base peak | new peak | PostgreSQL ops/s | base ops/s | new ops/s | base / new CPU ms/op |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 10 | 172 | 730 | 577 | 170 | 38 | 32 | 67 / 70 |
| 50 | 415 | 2,784 | 1,603 | 175 | 21 | 21 | 97 / 92 |
| 100 | 711 | 5,493 | 2,941 | 175 | 32 | 28 | 81 / 80 |
| 200 | 1,398 | 5,234 | 3,677 | 161 | 20 | 16 | 76 / 93 |

The grouping now holds about 230 bytes a group (key once, 24-byte states, the hash table's
order model in 8-byte slots, no 16 bytes per input row) instead of about 900. PostgreSQL
doesn't spill this grouping (50,000 groups fit hash_mem), and the engine doesn't either. What
is left of the engine's peak is mostly the sequential scan collecting the table's moved rows to
return them in heap order (`scan.rs` `next_raw`, agent 130's: about 30 MB a session here), and
the CPU per operation (80 ms against PostgreSQL's 20) is that scan and the grouping's per-row
work, not memory. The plain mix (without sort and agg) and the `--reconnect 1` variant: peaks
and ops/s within the run-to-run noise of this machine (plain at 100 sessions 440 / 459 MB,
1,299 / 924 ops/s, at 50 sessions 975 / 879, no consistent direction across sizes);
PostgreSQL with `--reconnect 1` at 200 sessions ran out of connection slots ("too many
clients already") and is left out.

### pgbench (8 clients, prepared, 15 s, scale 1), `base` 41fc8981 (origin) against `new` (this batch on it), alternated 3 times

| | base | new |
|---|---:|---:|
| select-only, engine CPU µs / transaction | 53, 51, 52 | 51, 52, 51 |
| TPC-B, engine CPU µs / transaction | 429, 419, 415 | 422, 433, 414 |
| RSS after (MB) | 108-144 | 122-148 |

### Spills against PostgreSQL

`SET work_mem = '1MB'` over 200,000 rows of (int, md5 text) (the a132 goldens' shape), the
engine's EXPLAIN ANALYZE against PostgreSQL's:

| | PostgreSQL | engine |
|---|---|---|
| `ORDER BY t` | external merge, Disk: 9208kB | external merge |
| `GROUP BY t` (200,000 groups) | Batches: 17, Memory Usage: 2073kB, Disk Usage: 14984kB | Batches: 17, Memory Usage: 2073kB, Disk Usage: 9448kB |
| temp_file_limit = 1MB | ERROR 53400 | ERROR 53400 |

The engine's own spill files are its row encodings (`storage::row`) with a length word,
written in 256 kB appends and read in 64 kB reads, not compressed (not measured against a
compressed variant yet). Disk Usage above is the model of PostgreSQL's tapes
without its preallocation (`ltsGetPreallocBlock` grows a partition's blocks 8 -> 128 at a time,
and the holes count), hence lower.

Not measured this round, left for the next: TPC-H SF1 before / after (the analytic queries'
sorts of 2M rows now spill at the default work_mem as PostgreSQL's do; their time and peak
against base), the difftest process's growth over 150 seeds (agent 118's), and the pgbench
simple-update peak at 64 clients after 57d7da04 (agent 126's).

## Size models, round 3 (round 23, agent 142)

HANDOFF.md, "Size models, round 3", has what changed. Base is 57d7da04, head is this batch.
These are release builds. The engine ran on cores 24-27 and pgbench (`-M prepared`, a
container on the host network) on 28-31, everything under `nice -n 19`, at scale 10, 20 s
per run after a 5 s warm-up. The engine was restarted per run on one data directory, with
base and head interleaved. Load was 40-50. Wall times and tps moved by 15% between
identical runs, so CPU per transaction (the engine's utime + stime) is the figure to read.

**Model memory and the first statement after a clean restart.** `tests/restart_cost.rs`:
300,000 rows of pgbench_accounts' shape with its primary key, a tenth updated, then a clean
restart. The test now counts live allocations. Two interleaved rounds:

| | base | head |
|---|---|---|
| live after the first statement (models laid out) | 74.4 MB | 46.9 MB |
| per row | ~237 bytes | ~145 bytes |
| first statement (point SELECT, waits for the layout) | 0.39 / 0.48 s | 0.29 / 0.35 s |
| process CPU | 0.62 / 0.80 s | 0.78 / 0.56 s |
| VmHWM | 144 / 144 MB | 116 / 136 MB |

PostgreSQL's pages for these rows: 44 MB heap, 6.8 MB index. The intermediate step (key
arena plus 32-byte line pointers, before thin btree keys) measured 56.9 MB.

**pgbench, 8 clients**:

| workload | base tps | head tps | base CPU ms/txn | head CPU ms/txn | base VmHWM | head VmHWM |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| select-only | 60,809 / 61,355 / 53,946 | 61,543 / 53,188 / 54,213 | 0.063 / 0.063 / 0.069 | 0.063 / 0.069 / 0.069 | 151 / 187 / 149 MB | 182 / 145 / 148 MB |
| simple-update | 11,733 | 12,160 | 0.266 | 0.258 | 262 MB | 244 MB |
| tpcb-like | 6,126 | 6,361 | 0.474 | 0.479 | 239 MB | 230 MB |

At scale 10, pgbench_accounts is past the 400,000-row limit, so only tellers, branches and
history are modelled. Nothing moved beyond the noise.

**Page counts against PostgreSQL 18.6**: round 2's mixed workload (the script above, with
mw1's row count as a parameter, mw2 at 2/3 of it). It reports pages before and after a
clean restart, then inserts and their ctids before and after a VACUUM:

| rows | differs from PostgreSQL |
|---|---|
| 60,000 | `mw1_v` 721 against 725 (as in round 2); all else equal (main / fsm / vm, 1,100 ctids, EXPLAIN costs) |
| 300,000 | `mw1_k` 485 against 487, `mw1_v` 3,696 against 3,706; all else equal |

Base's output at 300,000 rows is identical to head's.

## Monitoring scrapes (round 23, agent 128)

What changed (HANDOFF "Server monitoring and logging, round 4"): statistics reads under
stats_fetch_consistency = cache stopped copying every cached entry per call (quadratic over
a scan of pg_stat_*_tables), the consistency setting is derived once, plus the monitoring
features (proc array, parallel worker rows, checkpoint and temp-file numbers) whose cost is
below.

Method: release builds; the engine pinned to CPUs 20-23 (`taskset`), the reference
(PostgreSQL 18.6, the pinned image, pg_stat_statements preloaded, max_connections 200 so
100 sessions and the exporter fit) to 24-27 (`docker update --cpuset-cpus`); database
`scale` with 10,000 tables (a primary key each, 50 rows in every hundredth); 100 sessions
held open on each by `pgbench -c 100 -f idle.sql` (`\sleep 5 s; SELECT 1`);
prometheus-community postgres_exporter 0.17.1 (default collectors) on each, scraped with
curl, engine and reference in turn, 4 rounds. CPU: the engine process's utime + stime, the
reference container's cgroup usage_usec, over the scrape. The machine ran at load 40-55 on
36 CPUs (other agents' builds), so wall times moved by ±20% between rounds.

| per scrape (4 rounds) | PostgreSQL 18.6 | engine |
|---|---|---|
| wall | 6.0-8.1 s | 4.3-5.0 s |
| server CPU | 3.9-5.9 s | 2.1-2.5 s |
| series | 280,581 | 280,581 |
| memory (after the scrapes) | 330 MB (container) | 171 MB RSS, 273 MB peak |

Before the read fix the engine's scrape took 367-514 s (and 304-388 s of CPU): the
stat_user_tables and statio_user_tables collectors' views, 147 s and 52 s each. A scrape
every 15 s now costs the engine about 15% of one CPU, PostgreSQL's about 30%; most of
PostgreSQL's is pg_total_relation_size over every table (5.4 s alone; the engine's 0.8 s).

Views alone, database `scale`, one statement each (psql \timing, same load):

| statement | PostgreSQL | engine before | engine now |
|---|---|---|---|
| `count(*) FROM pg_stat_user_tables` | 59 ms | 147 s | 0.9 s |
| `count(*) FROM pg_statio_user_tables` | 16 ms | 52 s | 1.0-1.1 s |
| `sum(pg_total_relation_size(relid)) FROM pg_stat_user_tables` | 5,413 ms | 60 s | 0.7-0.8 s |
| `count(*) FROM pg_stat_user_indexes` | 42 ms | 8.1 s | 0.8 s |
| `count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity` (100 sessions) | 2.3 ms | 2.3 ms | 7 ms |
| `count(*) FROM pg_locks` | 0.8 ms | 0.4 ms | 0.8 ms |

The tools' own queries (`tests/clients/suites/monitoring.py`: pgwatch 3.7.0, pg_activity
3.6.1, pganalyze-collector 0.66.1, Datadog 22.12.0, check_postgres; 193 queries, median of
three runs each) in database `scale`, total per tool:

| tool | PostgreSQL | engine |
|---|---|---|
| pgwatch | 2,859 ms | 36,307 ms (30,006 of it table_stats_approx, at its 30 s timeout) |
| pganalyze | 534 ms | 87 ms |
| Datadog | 17 ms | 36 ms |
| check_postgres | 6 ms | 21 ms |
| pg_activity | 4.8 ms | 4.9 ms |

pgwatch's table_stats_approx is planned with a hash join inside a correlated sub-select over
pg_class / pg_index (rows=201 for pg_class's relkind = 'r' where PostgreSQL's estimate is
10,075): a planner estimate, left to the planner's owner. Its other per-table metrics run
2-4x PostgreSQL's time (table_stats 878 vs 267 ms, index_stats 732 vs 160 ms).

pgbench (scale 10, 8 clients, 20 s, 6 interleaved rounds per build, client on CPUs 28-31;
engine CPU per transaction, which load moves less than tps): select-only 260-307 µs base,
269-316 µs this round (medians 294 / 288); tpcb-like 1,731-2,238 µs base, 1,578-2,192 µs
(medians 1,795 / 1,816); peak RSS 142-220 MB base, 143-223 MB. No regression beyond the
noise of a machine at load 50.

Left: pg_stat_user_tables is still 15x PostgreSQL's per row (25 pg_stat_get_* calls per
row through the evaluator, each cloning the table's entry); pgwatch's table_stats_approx plan.

## Query memory, round 2 (round 24, agent 150)

HANDOFF.md, "Query memory, round 2", has what changed. Release builds with frame pointers
(`--profile release`, `-C force-frame-pointers=yes`, line tables), engine and PostgreSQL
18.6 each pinned to the same four cores (12-15) and both at nice 19 (PostgreSQL started as
`nice -n 19 docker-entrypoint.sh postgres -c max_connections=300`, its other settings the
defaults), on a machine other agents' builds kept 60-95% busy: absolute numbers vary by
10-20% between runs, so engine binaries are compared alternated (A, B, A, B). Engine
memory is its RSS, PostgreSQL's the container's anon + shmem. Logs:
`~/.cache/a150/logs` (copied numbers below).

### The 200-session burst (`bench burst --sessions 200 --secs 20`, scale 2)

| | ops/s | CPU µs / op | peak MB | engine / PostgreSQL |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| PostgreSQL 18.6 | 2,727 | 1,419 | 695 | |
| engine at `8115f5af` | 1,683 | 1,921 | 590 | 0.62x ops, 1.35x CPU, 0.85x memory |
| engine at `8115f5af` with the three changes below | 1,863 | 1,866 | 600 | 0.68x ops, 1.31x CPU, 0.86x memory |

The engine leads on memory and trails on CPU per operation and on how many of the four
cores it keeps busy (3.2-3.4 under `perf stat`, 12-14k context switches a second). Each
operation of the mix alone (200 sessions, 10 s, origin; CPU µs / op, engine against
PostgreSQL): `pl` (PL/pgSQL loop of ten key lookups) 654 / 320, `fn` (100 calls of a SQL
function) 2,980 / 2,116, `orm` (four-table nested loop join) 3,797 / 1,102, `cat` (psql's
and information_schema's column queries) 3,161 / 4,737 (the engine ahead), `tpcb` 1,647 /
1,586.

What this batch takes out (profiles of the engine under `pgbench -M prepared -c 8`):

- `parallel_part::gathers` ran the EXPLAIN builder's costing of the whole plan at every
  execution, to place PostgreSQL's Gathers: 27% of the ORM join's CPU. A per-thread cache
  of them by plan (while the catalog version, the settings and the relation sizes the
  costing read held) took the ORM join from 1,308-1,423 to 879-939 µs per execution,
  alternated three times (PostgreSQL 355-445); agent 145's e8663d91, which keeps them with
  the bound plan, landed first and replaces it.
- PL/pgSQL formatted a cache key of the routine's body, signature and environment for every
  expression it evaluated (12.5% of the `pl` operation, `compile` 21% in all): plans are now
  found by function, expression and a hash of what can change during or between calls
  (`compile` 4% of the operation).
- Text results of text, integer, boolean and oid columns go into the DataRow without a
  String each (`server::put_text_fast`).

Where the rest goes (shares of the engine's CPU in the profiles):

- size-model upkeep on reads (`sizing::fetched`, `Kills::mark` / `done`, `row_roots`,
  `heapsim::locate_for`, `keymap::find`, `btsim::kill_needed`): about 20% of the ORM join,
  8% of the `fn` operation (agent 151);
- planning of simple-protocol statements the kept plans don't cover (`fn`'s and `orm`'s
  literals, the catalog queries): 22% of the burst, with the EXPLAIN builder's estimates
  for `presorted_keys` (5.6%) and the Gathers of plans seen once (2.8%) (agents 145, 140);
- opening a SQL function's plan per call (19% of `fn`), allocation (`_mi_page_malloc_zero`
  7-15%, mostly decoded values and output text), the wire's text output (8-12%).

### TPC-H SF1 and the analytic set (2M rows) against PostgreSQL, before and after hash join batches

Measured 2026-11-01 (servers on cores 24-27, nice 19 for the engine): `base` 7dab0879 (round
1), `new` the hash join batches (c4746101's code), PostgreSQL with `shared_buffers=2GB`
(`BENCH.md` "Results" settings), the engine with `--cache-mb 2048`, sums of the medians of 2

On 2026-11-03 the engine at `8115f5af` spent 44-53 s on its first q01 after a start, with a

of its own), dropping those rows 7%; after packing: `encode_value` 3%, drops 3%. What's left:

**Where the peak comes from** (base, a fresh engine per cache size, 2 reps, CPU of both):

| --cache-mb | peak MB | CPU s | sum of medians s |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 2048 | 1,718 | 88.2 | 18.9 |
| 1024 | 1,509 | 131.4 | 29.6 |

Per query at 512 against 2048 (CPU s): q01 4.36 / 4.32 (a sequential scan of lineitem
costs no more uncached), q06 2.47 / 1.72, q09 22.59 / 4.89, q07 6.60 / 1.63, q17 6.69 /
1.90, q21 6.05 / 2.54, q02 2.83 / 1.05, q11 1.78 / 0.23, q20 1.57 / 0.39: the cost is index
lookups and joins reading blocks the cache no longer holds, a 16 kB block decompressed and
decoded per row fetched.

**Bulk admission** (new, a knob for the experiment only, not kept: the share of the cache a
bulk read may fill; 2 reps): 100 % 1,715 MB, 114.0 s CPU; 25 % 1,729 MB, 116.8 s; 0 %
1,732 MB, 117.7 s. Keeping big sequential scans out of the cache (PostgreSQL's ring) doesn't
lower the peak: lineitem comes in through q04 / q21's lookups by l_orderkey and q17 / q20's by
l_partkey, as PostgreSQL's shared buffers take it in at q03 (its peak goes 115 MB after q01,
1,342 MB after q03). PostgreSQL's lineitem is 879 MB of heap, the engine's decoded blocks
about 970 MB.

## Where it is still slower, and why

In rough order of what it costs users:

1. **Analytic queries and TPC-H (5–20x slower in round 17; 1.7–10x since round 18, which
   did the first steps below: see its section).** The executor pulls one `Row` (a
   `Vec<Value>`, a heap allocation per text value) at a time through boxed nodes, evaluates
   expressions by walking `Expr` trees, aggregates `numeric` as `BigInt`s, and runs every plan
   on one thread (PostgreSQL ran two parallel workers on the same four cores). A scan
   materialises the table to put it in heap order and decodes every column of every row,
   needed or not. The fixes, biggest first: decode only the columns a plan reads (a
   projection mask passed down to scans), a batch (vector) interface between scans, filters,
   projections and aggregates with typed column arrays, `i128` / scaled-integer numeric
   accumulators, and running a plan's Gather over worker threads.
2. **Bulk loads, CREATE INDEX, DELETE, VACUUM, ANALYZE (3–10x slower).** Round 18 (agent 43)
   streamed the index builds, VACUUM, ANALYZE and ALTER TABLE's checks, and ingests big
   commits as SSTs. Round 19 (agent 69) bounded the memory: a transaction's write buffer
   spills to sorted runs on disk, COPY FROM reads its data a batch at a time, and index builds
   write sorted runs (the 1M bulk run's peak 1.75 GB → 0.85 GB). Round 19 (agent 74) defers
   a big statement's heap model, parses and prints COPY data without a copy per field and
   floats by Ryu, and keeps bulk locks compact (COPY in 2x, COPY out 2.5x; a 5M-row INSERT …
   SELECT 2.6 GB → 0.5 GB). What is left:
   - Per-row executor and storage work dominates the time. COPY spends ~2.4 µs a row against
     PostgreSQL's ~0.8: a row goes through `Expr` constants and `InsertNode`'s general path
     (a `Frame` per row, defaults, checks), a `BTreeMap` buffer insert with its journal entry
     (the key copied twice), the sorted run it spills to and the commit's SST it is merged
     into (each with a bloom filter bit set per key, cache misses on a big filter). DELETE
     writes a tombstone per row and per index entry (Postgres sets xmax only) and checks the
     transaction's own writes twice per row.
   - Locks: compact past 2,048 per transaction (`txn/bulklocks.rs`, about 20 bytes plus the
     key). What a big UPDATE or DELETE still holds per row is the write buffer's (spilled)
     and, over a join, the set of rows done.
   - ANALYZE of a table too big to model measures every row for the planner's relpages and
     index pages: the text width of every value and an index sort key per row, then sorted.
     The sample itself is cheap (agent 64 samples by pages). Estimating those from the sample
     would change relpages (TPC-H plans read them; agent 64). CREATE INDEX measures the same
     way (`measure_rows`), repeating its own build's key extraction and sort.
   - An index build writes its entries twice: as sorted runs, then into the commit's SST.
     Linking runs in as L0 files would need a per-file commit timestamp in the SST format.
3. **Index scans over many rows (range scans, secondary lookups: 0.2–0.4x at 8 clients over
   the wire in round 17).** Round 18 (agent 43) batched an index scan's heap fetches and
   answers index-only scans of big tables from the entries (1.2–1.4x in-process). What is
   left is per row: each fetched row is decoded into a `Vec<Value>` and goes through the
   scan node's run and statistics bookkeeping; over the wire the protocol encoding adds as
   much again.
4. **DDL at scale.** Since round 18's agent 44 the catalog is stored per object, and since
   agent 49 its maps are structurally shared with secondary indexes kept on mutation ("Catalog
   and DDL at scale" above): at 10,000 tables CREATE TABLE is 2.4x PostgreSQL's speed, CREATE
   INDEX and COMMENT about even, and restoring an 11,000-table schema dump 2x faster than
   PostgreSQL; ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN (0.18x) and DROP TABLE (0.25x) are still slower.
5. **Catalog introspection (`\d`, ORMs).** Rows are shared per catalog version and looked up
   through hashes (above): 1.3–2.5 ms for psql's `\d` queries at 500 tables, ORM constraint
   queries ahead of PostgreSQL; the first query after DDL regenerates a catalog whole.
6. **Multi-client writes (0.6–0.8x at 8 clients over the wire, ahead in-process).** CPU per
   statement is about twice PostgreSQL's under concurrency: the remaining profile is flat
   (allocation, protocol, binding, lock-manager waits on hot rows).
7. **First read of a big table after the database opens.** The heap model is decided on
   first use: a table past `MAX_VERSIONS` is read once to estimate its size (before: every
   row held in memory, then dropped; now the first 400k decoded and the rest's stored lengths
   summed, nothing held). Persisting the estimate with the table would remove the read. (The
   in-process first `count(*)` of lineitem took 11.5 s in round 16 (a sequential scan of a
   table the planner thought empty), 37–51 s in round 17 (an index-only scan fetching every
   row) and 6.6–18 s since round 18, agent 43: 1–8 s of it this read, 5.7 s the index-only scan
   from the entries. PostgreSQL plans a parallel index-only scan of `l_shipdate`'s index
   there; the engine's estimate of the big table's indexes picks another, so that EXPLAIN
   still differs.)
8. **Memory per idle session**: 0.5 MB since round 18's agent 44 (PostgreSQL 1.5 MB; it was
   2–3 MB: transparent huge pages under mimalloc's per-thread heaps, and per-session catalog
   rows).

Where the engine is ahead: single-client latency everywhere in OLTP (no process per
connection, no shared-buffer locking, statement plans cached), select-only and TPC-B pgbench
up to 32 clients at scale 1, read-mostly multi-client point selects, connection setup (3x),
on-disk size (2.4x smaller: zstd-compressed SSTs), and, embedded, everything OLTP (no
protocol: 2–27x PostgreSQL over the wire).

## Verification

- `cargo test -p pg-engine` (all of it) and `cargo clippy -p pg-engine --all-targets` (no
  new warnings) after every batch and after the rebase.
- The regression suite in full, with its own reference container, for this round's start
  (`b1917d6`) and its end before the rebase: **51,129 / 51,150 both, identical per file**
  (`tests/regress/REPORT.md` format, compared file by file).
- New tests: `storage::tests::reverse_scan_matches_forward` (descending scans, seeks over
  many versions, pruning under pinned snapshots, against a model of every version written),
  `exec::fasthash::tests::row_keys_spread`, `tests/wire.rs` `text_parameters_of_reg_types`,
  `tests/session_state.rs` `catalog_rows_follow_ddl_roles_and_settings`.
- Every benchmark query's result rows are hashed and compared with PostgreSQL's (above).
- After the final rebase: agent 35's differential tester, `difftest smoke`, passes.
- Storage format: unchanged (no new keys or value layouts); WAL files are zero-filled past
  their last record, which this version's replay stops at. (An older binary replaying a WAL
  this version left after a crash fails on the zeros: downgrades after a crash aren't
  supported. Round 18, agent 43, made that the rule: see its "Durability" section.)
- Round 18, agent 44: `cargo test -p pg-engine` and clippy (no new warnings) on the tree as
  committed; the regression suite in full, each run with a reference cluster of its own
  (HANDOFF, "A private reference per run"), for the base (`44adff0`) and for the change:

  bases, `ca0c077`, `6dd2469` and `948d0e8`, against the change at the time: identical too);
  `difftest smoke` passes. New tests: `tests/catalog_storage.rs` (the catalog
  after restarts, rollbacks and savepoints, after a crash replayed from the WAL, a catalog of
  the old format rewritten, the bytes DDL writes not growing with the catalog, and
  a merged concurrent DDL commit stored whole), `tests/session_state.rs`
  `catalog_rows_shared_by_sessions_follow_their_state` (sessions of
  other settings, roles, paths and temporary schemas reading the shared rows),
  `tests/wire.rs` `idle_hook_runs_and_the_connection_goes_on`, golden `catalog_lookups`
